Foundation

Foundation

Who to target and how to build the list before a single email goes out.

Play 01 · Foundation

ICP from Won Deals

Trigger Closed-won and closed-lost deals from the last 12 to 24 months plus the best 10 customers
Outcome A buildable ICP every list can be tested against
Channels email

Give this to your agent 
Install the Pipeline.tech "ICP from Won Deals" outbound play. Fetch
https://raw.githubusercontent.com/pipeline-tech/plays/main/core/SKILL.md and
https://raw.githubusercontent.com/pipeline-tech/plays/main/plays/icp-from-won-deals/SKILL.md,
save them as ~/.claude/skills/pipeline-outbound-core/SKILL.md and
~/.claude/skills/pipeline-icp-from-won-deals/SKILL.md, then run /pipeline-icp-from-won-deals.
Paste into Claude Code. It installs the shared core rules plus this play, then runs it as /pipeline-icp-from-won-deals.
Workflow, generated from the skillOpen full size

"B2B SaaS, 50 to 500 people" is a market, not an ICP, and a list built on it exhausts the budget before it finds the buyer. The real ICP is the exact company and persona most likely to buy and stay, and your closed-won deals already describe it while your closed-lost deals describe the edges. This play reads both, writes the ICP as five parts a list builder can act on, and proves it by backtesting against the deals you already closed.

Load pipeline-outbound-core first. Where a step says "apply core", follow that section of the core skill.

Inputs

Ask for these before step 1, and save them to ./pipeline-plays.config.json:

  1. CRM access (Attio first, HubSpot as the option) or a CSV export of deals. Stack answers from core section 1.
  2. The window. Default 24 months, never less than 12. Under 30 closed-won deals, say so and widen the window or add current customers with their start date as the close date.
  3. The best 10 customers by revenue, retention and ease of close. If the user cannot name them, rank by annual value times months retained and show the top 15 for a hand pick.
  4. Planned send volume per month. This sets the TAM floor in step 4.
  5. What the product does in one sentence, so the model can judge which function owns the problem.

Steps

1Export the deals

ToolsAttioHubSpot (option)CSV
  • Pull every closed-won and closed-lost deal in the window with value, cycle length in days, close date, stage reached, the loss reason as the rep wrote it, and the first contact's title.
  • Resolve a domain for every company in its own pass. A row with no domain cannot be enriched or backtested.
  • Write one CSV with an outcome column. Keep the raw export untouched beside it.

2Enrich the companies

ToolsOcean.ioApolloBuiltWithAI Ark (optional)
  • Add firmographics to every row, then technographics from the website. The stack says more about buying behaviour than the industry label does.
  • Add the 12-month headcount change and open roles in the function you sell to. These are the trigger candidates for step 4.

3Find the patterns

ToolsClaude
  • Give the model the enriched file and ask for three lists: traits far more common in won than in lost, traits far more common in lost than in won, and loss reasons that repeat three times or more. Every claim cites the count on each side.
  • Reverse-engineer the best 10 customers one at a time: headcount at signing, who signed, what the company was doing that quarter, the stack at the time. What was true before they bought is the ICP. What is true now is the result of being a customer.
  • Where the 10 disagree with the aggregate, trust the 10. They are the customers you want more of.

4Write the five buildable parts

ToolsClaudeGoogle Sheets
  • Write the ICP as five parts a list builder can filter on: a revenue or headcount floor, the function that owns the problem, a trigger visible this quarter, the disqualifiers written out, and a TAM large enough for the planned send volume. A 400-company ICP exhausts in six weeks.
  • Define the decision maker per company size band. Apply core: enrichment for the bands, then name the titles the won deals actually signed with inside each band.
  • Run the one-message test. Draft one 40-word opener. If it fits every company in the segment, the segment holds. If some companies need a different first sentence, the segment splits and each half gets its own five parts.
  • Count the TAM. If it covers fewer than four months of planned volume, loosen the floor or the trigger, never the disqualifiers.

5Backtest against the deals

ToolsClaudeCSV
  • Score every company in the deal file against the written ICP with the outcome column hidden. The scorer sees only the enrichment and the website.
  • Target: 95 percent or more of closed-won pass. Losses should cluster outside the ICP, or inside it with the same loss reason. A loss that passes with a unique reason is a sales problem, not an ICP problem, so leave it.
  • Below 95 percent, read the won deals that failed. If they share a trait, the ICP is too narrow on that trait. If they share nothing, they are exceptions and the ICP stands.

6Score any list against it

ToolsSerperFirecrawlClaude
  • Pull the homepage and one product or about page per company. Search results fill the gap when a site will not load.
  • A cheap model scores each company 0 to 100 against the five parts with one reason. 80 and above is strong, 60 to 79 likely, below 60 is out. Pass at 60. A company matching a disqualifier scores 0 regardless.
  • Read the drop reasons in bulk. One reason repeating across many rows means the ICP wording is wrong, not the list. Hand-sample 50 keeps.
  • Apply core: lists for the sourcing order. This step runs before any paid people lookup.

7Save and review quarterly

ToolsCSVGoogle SheetsAttio
  • Write ./icp.md with the five parts, the decision maker per size band, the disqualifiers, the backtest numbers and the date. Write the same content as structured fields into ./pipeline-plays.config.json under icp, so the signal plays and list plays filter on it without asking.
  • Every 90 days, export the deals closed since the last review and rerun step 5 on them alone. Change the ICP only when the pass rate drops or a new loss reason repeats.
  • Apply core: credits and handoff.

Guardrails

  • Never write an industry label as the ICP. "SaaS" or "e-commerce" is a market. The five parts are the ICP.
  • Never let the model see the outcome column during the backtest or the list scoring. A scorer that knows the answer proves nothing.
  • Never loosen a disqualifier to hit the TAM number. Loosen the floor or the trigger instead.
  • Never carry customer names, deal values or loss reasons into any file a prospect could see. The ICP is the pattern, not the roster.
  • Fewer than 30 closed-won deals is a thin sample. Say so in icp.md and schedule the first review at 60 days instead of 90.

Example run

User: "/pipeline-icp-from-won-deals, we sell a revenue intelligence tool, Attio is the CRM"

  1. Exported 24 months from Attio: 68 closed-won, 214 closed-lost, 11 rows with no domain fixed by hand.
  2. Enriched 282 companies. Headcount, revenue band and stack found for 271.
  3. Four win traits: 80 to 400 headcount, a VP of Sales or CRO in seat under 18 months, an outbound stack running, at least one open SDR role. "No budget" and "too early" repeated 61 times, almost all under 40 people.
  4. Best 10 agreed on three of the four and added a Series A or B in the prior 12 months. Wrote the five parts, set the floor at 80 headcount and the trigger as an SDR or AE role opened this quarter. The one-message test split the segment into mid-market with a CRO and SMB with a founder-led sales team.
  5. Backtest: 65 of 68 won passed (96 percent); 183 of 214 lost failed, 24 of the rest shared the loss reason "already using a competitor".
  6. Scored a 2.4K-company Apollo pull: 1.1K passed at 60, 390 strong. Drop reason "no outbound motion" repeated 600 times, so the ICP gained a stack requirement.
  7. Saved icp.md and the config, next review booked for 90 days out.
The skill fileplays/icp-from-won-deals/SKILL.mdExactly what gets installed. Copy it, save it as ~/.claude/skills/pipeline-icp-from-won-deals/SKILL.md next to the core skill, and run /pipeline-icp-from-won-deals.

Exports 12 to 24 months of deals, enriches the companies, finds shared win traits and loss traits with a model, reverse-engineers the best 10 customers, writes the ICP as five buildable parts, backtests it against the deals, and saves it so every other play reads it. USE WHEN user says "/pipeline-icp-from-won-deals" OR "ICP from won deals" OR "build our ICP" OR "define the ICP" OR "who should we target" OR "backtest the ICP" OR "score this list against the ICP".

requires: pipeline-outbound-core
trigger: Closed-won and closed-lost deals from the last 12 to 24 months plus the best 10 customers

# ICP from Won Deals

"B2B SaaS, 50 to 500 people" is a market, not an ICP, and a list built on it exhausts the budget before it finds the buyer. The real ICP is the exact company and persona most likely to buy and stay, and your closed-won deals already describe it while your closed-lost deals describe the edges. This play reads both, writes the ICP as five parts a list builder can act on, and proves it by backtesting against the deals you already closed.

Load `pipeline-outbound-core` first. Where a step says "apply core", follow that section of the core skill.

## Inputs

Ask for these before step 1, and save them to `./pipeline-plays.config.json`:

1. CRM access (Attio first, HubSpot as the option) or a CSV export of deals. Stack answers from core section 1.
2. The window. Default 24 months, never less than 12. Under 30 closed-won deals, say so and widen the window or add current customers with their start date as the close date.
3. The best 10 customers by revenue, retention and ease of close. If the user cannot name them, rank by annual value times months retained and show the top 15 for a hand pick.
4. Planned send volume per month. This sets the TAM floor in step 4.
5. What the product does in one sentence, so the model can judge which function owns the problem.

## Steps

### 1. Export the deals
Tools: Attio, HubSpot (option), CSV
Flow:
- Closed-won | Every won deal in the window with value, cycle length, close date and the first contact's title | with: Attio, HubSpot | chips: value, cycle days, close date, first title
- Closed-lost | Every lost deal in the window with the loss reason as written by the rep | with: Attio, HubSpot | chips: loss reason, stage reached, days open
= One row per deal | Won and lost in one file so the model sees both sides | with: CSV | chips: outcome, domain, value, cycle, reason

- Pull every closed-won and closed-lost deal in the window with value, cycle length in days, close date, stage reached, the loss reason as the rep wrote it, and the first contact's title.
- Resolve a domain for every company in its own pass. A row with no domain cannot be enriched or backtested.
- Write one CSV with an `outcome` column. Keep the raw export untouched beside it.

### 2. Enrich the companies
Tools: Ocean.io, Apollo, BuiltWith, AI Ark (optional)
Flow:
- Firmographics | Headcount, revenue band, industry, founding year, geography, funding stage on every row | with: Ocean.io, Apollo
- Technographics | The stack the company runs, since a tool on the site often predicts the buyer better than the industry | with: BuiltWith | chips: CRM, sending tool, data vendor, analytics
- Growth marks | Headcount change over 12 months and open roles in the owning function | with: AI Ark, Apollo | tag: dated

- Add firmographics to every row, then technographics from the website. The stack says more about buying behaviour than the industry label does.
- Add the 12-month headcount change and open roles in the function you sell to. These are the trigger candidates for step 4.

### 3. Find the patterns
Tools: Claude
Flow:
- Win traits | What the won deals share, ranked by how much more often it appears in won than in lost | with: Claude | chips: headcount band, function, stack, trigger
- Loss traits and negative indicators | What the lost deals share that the won deals lack, plus loss reasons that repeat | with: Claude | tag: disqualifiers
= Best 10 reverse-engineered | What was true at each of the 10 customers before they bought, not after | with: Claude | chips: size at signing, who signed, what changed that quarter

- Give the model the enriched file and ask for three lists: traits far more common in won than in lost, traits far more common in lost than in won, and loss reasons that repeat three times or more. Every claim cites the count on each side.
- Reverse-engineer the best 10 customers one at a time: headcount at signing, who signed, what the company was doing that quarter, the stack at the time. What was true before they bought is the ICP. What is true now is the result of being a customer.
- Where the 10 disagree with the aggregate, trust the 10. They are the customers you want more of.

### 4. Write the five buildable parts
Tools: Claude, Google Sheets
Flow:
- Floor and function | A revenue or headcount floor and the function that owns the problem | with: Claude | chips: floor, owning function
- Trigger and disqualifiers | One this-quarter trigger a list can be filtered on, and the disqualifiers written out | with: Claude | tag: core: exclusions
- TAM check | Companies matching the first four parts, counted against planned send volume | with: Google Sheets | chips: companies, contacts, months of volume
= One-message test | One message must fit every company in the segment or the segment splits | with: Claude | tag: split or pass

- Write the ICP as five parts a list builder can filter on: a revenue or headcount floor, the function that owns the problem, a trigger visible this quarter, the disqualifiers written out, and a TAM large enough for the planned send volume. A 400-company ICP exhausts in six weeks.
- Define the decision maker per company size band. Apply core: enrichment for the bands, then name the titles the won deals actually signed with inside each band.
- Run the one-message test. Draft one 40-word opener. If it fits every company in the segment, the segment holds. If some companies need a different first sentence, the segment splits and each half gets its own five parts.
- Count the TAM. If it covers fewer than four months of planned volume, loosen the floor or the trigger, never the disqualifiers.

### 5. Backtest against the deals
Tools: Claude, CSV
Flow:
- Score every deal | Each won and lost company scored against the written ICP without seeing its outcome | with: Claude | chips: score, pass or fail, reason
= Two numbers | Share of closed-won that pass and share of closed-lost that fail; 95 percent and a clear cluster, or the ICP goes back a step | with: CSV | tag: 95 percent won pass

- Score every company in the deal file against the written ICP with the outcome column hidden. The scorer sees only the enrichment and the website.
- Target: 95 percent or more of closed-won pass. Losses should cluster outside the ICP, or inside it with the same loss reason. A loss that passes with a unique reason is a sales problem, not an ICP problem, so leave it.
- Below 95 percent, read the won deals that failed. If they share a trait, the ICP is too narrow on that trait. If they share nothing, they are exceptions and the ICP stands.

### 6. Score any list against it
Tools: Serper, Firecrawl, Claude
Flow:
- Website read | Homepage and one product page per company, pulled cheap | with: Serper, Firecrawl
- Score 0 to 100 | A cheap model reads the site and the ICP, returns a score, a label and one reason | with: Claude | chips: 80 plus strong, 60 to 79 likely, under 60 out | tag: pass at 60

- Pull the homepage and one product or about page per company. Search results fill the gap when a site will not load.
- A cheap model scores each company 0 to 100 against the five parts with one reason. 80 and above is strong, 60 to 79 likely, below 60 is out. Pass at 60. A company matching a disqualifier scores 0 regardless.
- Read the drop reasons in bulk. One reason repeating across many rows means the ICP wording is wrong, not the list. Hand-sample 50 keeps.
- Apply core: lists for the sourcing order. This step runs before any paid people lookup.

### 7. Save and review quarterly
Tools: CSV, Google Sheets, Attio
Flow:
- Two files | The ICP as prose and the same ICP as structured fields every other play reads | with: CSV, Google Sheets | chips: icp.md, pipeline-plays.config.json
= Quarterly review | Last 90 days of won and lost rescored; the ICP moves only when the numbers do | with: Attio | tag: every 90 days

- Write `./icp.md` with the five parts, the decision maker per size band, the disqualifiers, the backtest numbers and the date. Write the same content as structured fields into `./pipeline-plays.config.json` under `icp`, so the signal plays and list plays filter on it without asking.
- Every 90 days, export the deals closed since the last review and rerun step 5 on them alone. Change the ICP only when the pass rate drops or a new loss reason repeats.
- Apply core: credits and handoff.

## Guardrails

- Never write an industry label as the ICP. "SaaS" or "e-commerce" is a market. The five parts are the ICP.
- Never let the model see the outcome column during the backtest or the list scoring. A scorer that knows the answer proves nothing.
- Never loosen a disqualifier to hit the TAM number. Loosen the floor or the trigger instead.
- Never carry customer names, deal values or loss reasons into any file a prospect could see. The ICP is the pattern, not the roster.
- Fewer than 30 closed-won deals is a thin sample. Say so in `icp.md` and schedule the first review at 60 days instead of 90.

## Example run

User: "/pipeline-icp-from-won-deals, we sell a revenue intelligence tool, Attio is the CRM"

1. Exported 24 months from Attio: 68 closed-won, 214 closed-lost, 11 rows with no domain fixed by hand.
2. Enriched 282 companies. Headcount, revenue band and stack found for 271.
3. Four win traits: 80 to 400 headcount, a VP of Sales or CRO in seat under 18 months, an outbound stack running, at least one open SDR role. "No budget" and "too early" repeated 61 times, almost all under 40 people.
4. Best 10 agreed on three of the four and added a Series A or B in the prior 12 months. Wrote the five parts, set the floor at 80 headcount and the trigger as an SDR or AE role opened this quarter. The one-message test split the segment into mid-market with a CRO and SMB with a founder-led sales team.
5. Backtest: 65 of 68 won passed (96 percent); 183 of 214 lost failed, 24 of the rest shared the loss reason "already using a competitor".
6. Scored a 2.4K-company Apollo pull: 1.1K passed at 60, 390 strong. Drop reason "no outbound motion" repeated 600 times, so the ICP gained a stack requirement.
7. Saved `icp.md` and the config, next review booked for 90 days out.

Play 02 · Foundation

Full-TAM Map

Trigger An ICP file and at least two data sources
Outcome Every company that fits, with a count you can trust
Channels email

Give this to your agent 
Install the Pipeline.tech "Full-TAM Map" outbound play. Fetch
https://raw.githubusercontent.com/pipeline-tech/plays/main/core/SKILL.md and
https://raw.githubusercontent.com/pipeline-tech/plays/main/plays/full-tam-map/SKILL.md,
save them as ~/.claude/skills/pipeline-outbound-core/SKILL.md and
~/.claude/skills/pipeline-full-tam-map/SKILL.md, then run /pipeline-full-tam-map.
Paste into Claude Code. It installs the shared core rules plus this play, then runs it as /pipeline-full-tam-map.
Workflow, generated from the skillOpen full size

Every outbound number you read later sits on top of this list, so it has to be the whole market and not one vendor's slice of it. No single database sees every company, and vendor industry labels are wrong often enough that you cannot filter on them alone. Pull from at least two sources per segment, merge, read every website before you spend a credit, and you get a count to plan a year of campaigns on.

Load pipeline-outbound-core first. Where a step says "apply core", follow that section of the core skill.

Inputs

Ask for these before step 1, and save them to ./pipeline-plays.config.json:

  1. The ICP. Read ./icp.md if it exists (from ICP from Won Deals). Otherwise ask for one paragraph: sub-verticals, headcount band, geography, the problem you solve, who owns it, any must-have technology.
  2. Five to ten seed customers as domains, best accounts first.
  3. Stack answers from core section 1, plus which account databases and lookalike tools the user can access.
  4. Where the TAM should live: the CRM or a sheet.

Steps

1Split the ICP into segments

ToolsClaude
  • Read the ICP and write one row per segment: sub-vertical, headcount band, geography, buyer title, required or excluded technology.
  • For each segment, name the two or more sources that can see it. SaaS lives in Apollo and a lookalike tool; a local or licensed segment lives in Google Maps or a registry, where a database under-counts.
  • Assign the seeds to segments; a segment with none gets its lookalike pull later, from the first companies that qualify.

2Pull the account databases

ToolsApolloApifyCrunchbase (option)BuiltWith (option)
  • Apollo is the volume source. Pull each segment on industry, headcount and location filters; when the list is large, export through an Apollo scraper on Apify (about 0.20 USD per 1K rows) rather than credits.
  • Have Claude generate 20 to 30 title keyword variations for the buyer, informal and legacy titles included, and run them as a people search on the same company filters. This surfaces companies the industry label hid and gives a buyer count per company for step 7. Do not buy the contacts yet.
  • Pull niche databases where the segment lives: Crunchbase for funding stage and investors, BuiltWith for a required technology, StoreLeads for ecommerce brands, Clutch for agencies.
  • Every row gets a source column with the database name and the pull date.

3Expand from seed customers

ToolsOcean.ioAI Ark (option)DiscoLike (option)
  • Run Ocean.io first with 5 to 10 seed domains per segment at standard similarity (about 0.79), headcount and country set to the segment. Paginate to the end; the first page is not the market. Drop to 0.70 only when results are thin.
  • AI Ark is an alternative when the user has it. DiscoLike sources from Google rather than LinkedIn, so when its count sits far above the database count, the database is under-reporting that segment.
  • Keep the similarity score as a column for tiering later.

4Pull where databases cannot see

ToolsGoogle MapsSerperApifyFirecrawl
  • If a segment is local or owner-operated, query Google Maps by exact category and city and scrape the results. Zip code beats city where categories are dense. The Local TAM from Google Maps play has the full method.
  • Where the ICP is defined by a licence, a membership or a hiring pattern, go to the record itself: state licensing registries, association member lists, public ATS job feeds (Greenhouse, Lever and Ashby publish JSON). Serper finds the pages, Firecrawl or Apify fetch them. Pull broad, filter in step 6.
  • Rows that arrive without a domain get one in a separate pass (company name plus city into Serper, first non-directory result), since asking inline costs recall.

5Merge and dedupe

ToolsCSVGoogle SheetsClaude
  • Normalize domains (strip protocol, www and paths) and merge on domain first. Match the remaining rows on normalized company name plus state, then on phone.
  • When sources disagree on headcount or industry, keep both values in their own columns.
  • Write a sources column listing every source that saw the company, and a source_count.
  • Keep the raw pull from every source as its own file; the merged table is derived. Apply core: lists.
  • Read the overlap. Under 30 percent means a third source is worth the pull. Any single source's count is a floor.

6Qualify on the website, detect the stack

ToolsFirecrawlClaudeBuiltWith (option)
  • Fetch the homepage and about page of every merged company. Have a cheap model answer: does this company match the ICP, yes or no, one reason, and a confidence. This routinely removes 25 to 40 percent of a raw pull.
  • Keep the no's in the file with their verdict, out of the TAM count. A later ICP revision may want them.
  • If the ICP names a technology (an ecommerce platform, a CRM, a competitor), search the saved homepage HTML for its script and link patterns; BuiltWith is the paid option when the pattern is not visible.
  • Retry blocked and timed-out sites through a headless fetcher before marking them unknown; unknown stays in the TAM, flagged.
  • Apply core: exclusions, so customers, open deals and competitors leave before anything is sized.

7Size, write and schedule the refresh

ToolsClaudeAttioHubSpot (option)Google Sheets (option)
  • Multiply qualified companies by the buyer count from step 2 for a contact estimate per segment. Above 100K contacts, be aggressive and test broadly. Below 30K, be surgical: every email matters.
  • Expect to reach 60 to 80 percent of companies after enrichment. Plan campaign volume on that number, not on the TAM.
  • Write every company to the CRM (or a sheet) with columns: segment, sources, source_count, icp_verdict, reason, tech_detected, similarity, pulled_at.
  • Refresh in 60 days: re-run steps 2 to 4, merge new companies in, and flag those that no longer appear in any source.
  • Print the count per segment with the sources that fed it. Apply core: credits and handoff.

Guardrails

  • Never report one source's count as the market. Say "at least" until a second source has been merged.
  • Never spend a people-lookup credit on a company that has not passed the website read.
  • Never let a vendor industry label include or exclude a company on its own. The website decides.
  • A company without a domain is one to resolve, not one to drop.
  • This play ends at a company list. Contacts, enrichment and sending belong to the plays built on it.
  • A TAM older than 60 days is an estimate, not a map.

Example run

User: "/pipeline-full-tam-map, ICP is in ./icp.md: payroll and compliance software for US staffing agencies, 20 to 500 people"

  1. Read icp.md and split into two segments (light industrial staffing, healthcare staffing). Took 8 seed domains, confirmed Apollo, Ocean.io, DiscoLike and Attio.
  2. Apollo returned 6,400 companies on industry filters; the 24-title people pass added 1,900 more and a buyer count averaging 2.8 per company.
  3. Ocean.io returned 3,100 lookalikes at 0.79 across both segments. DiscoLike returned 4,700, which marked the database pull as a floor.
  4. Two association member lists added 900 rows, 740 after domain resolution.
  5. Merged to 12,300 unique domains, 11,800 after the name plus state and phone passes. Source overlap was 41 percent.
  6. 7,900 passed the website read (67 percent). 212 removed as customers, open deals or competitors. Tech detect not needed for this ICP.
  7. 7,688 companies, about 21,500 contacts, under 30K so surgical. Written to Attio with source columns, raw pulls kept, refresh set for 60 days.
The skill fileplays/full-tam-map/SKILL.mdExactly what gets installed. Copy it, save it as ~/.claude/skills/pipeline-full-tam-map/SKILL.md next to the core skill, and run /pipeline-full-tam-map.
---
name: pipeline-full-tam-map
description: Pipeline.tech play. Map every company that fits the ICP and get a count you can trust. Pulls each segment from at least two sources (account databases with generated title keywords, lookalike tools from seed customers, Google Maps and directories, public registries and ATS feeds), merges and dedupes by domain then name plus state then phone, qualifies every company from its own website before any paid people lookup, detects tech stack where the ICP needs it, sizes the result, and writes the TAM to the CRM with a source column and a 60-day refresh. USE WHEN user says "/pipeline-full-tam-map" OR "full TAM map" OR "map the TAM" OR "how big is our market" OR "build the full account list" OR "size the ICP".
author: Pipeline.tech (https://pipeline.tech/plays/full-tam-map)
version: 0.1.0
requires: pipeline-outbound-core
trigger: An ICP file and at least two data sources
outcome: Every company that fits, with a count you can trust
category: foundation
channels: email
tools: Apollo, Ocean.io, AI Ark (option), DiscoLike (option), Crunchbase (option), BuiltWith (option), Google Maps, Serper, Apify, Firecrawl, Claude, Attio, HubSpot (option), Google Sheets, CSV
---

# Full-TAM Map

Every outbound number you read later sits on top of this list, so it has to be the whole market and not one vendor's slice of it. No single database sees every company, and vendor industry labels are wrong often enough that you cannot filter on them alone. Pull from at least two sources per segment, merge, read every website before you spend a credit, and you get a count to plan a year of campaigns on.

Load `pipeline-outbound-core` first. Where a step says "apply core", follow that section of the core skill.

## Inputs

Ask for these before step 1, and save them to `./pipeline-plays.config.json`:

1. The ICP. Read `./icp.md` if it exists (from ICP from Won Deals). Otherwise ask for one paragraph: sub-verticals, headcount band, geography, the problem you solve, who owns it, any must-have technology.
2. Five to ten seed customers as domains, best accounts first.
3. Stack answers from core section 1, plus which account databases and lookalike tools the user can access.
4. Where the TAM should live: the CRM or a sheet.

## Steps

### 1. Split the ICP into segments
Tools: Claude
Flow:
= One row per segment | Sub-vertical, headcount band, geography, buyer title, must-have tech, and the sources that can see it | with: Claude | chips: segment, filters, sources, seeds

- Read the ICP and write one row per segment: sub-vertical, headcount band, geography, buyer title, required or excluded technology.
- For each segment, name the two or more sources that can see it. SaaS lives in Apollo and a lookalike tool; a local or licensed segment lives in Google Maps or a registry, where a database under-counts.
- Assign the seeds to segments; a segment with none gets its lookalike pull later, from the first companies that qualify.

### 2. Pull the account databases
Tools: Apollo, Apify, Crunchbase (option), BuiltWith (option)
Flow:
- Firmographic pull | Industry, headcount and location filters per segment, exported with every company field the database offers | with: Apollo, Apify | chips: name, domain, headcount, industry, location | tag: volume
- Title keyword pass | 20 to 30 generated title variations run as a people search on the same filters, to catch mislabelled companies and count buyers | with: Apollo, Claude | chips: titles, buyer count
- Niche databases | Funding and investors, tech stack, ecommerce or agency directories, where the segment lives there | with: Crunchbase, BuiltWith | tag: option

- Apollo is the volume source. Pull each segment on industry, headcount and location filters; when the list is large, export through an Apollo scraper on Apify (about 0.20 USD per 1K rows) rather than credits.
- Have Claude generate 20 to 30 title keyword variations for the buyer, informal and legacy titles included, and run them as a people search on the same company filters. This surfaces companies the industry label hid and gives a buyer count per company for step 7. Do not buy the contacts yet.
- Pull niche databases where the segment lives: Crunchbase for funding stage and investors, BuiltWith for a required technology, StoreLeads for ecommerce brands, Clutch for agencies.
- Every row gets a `source` column with the database name and the pull date.

### 3. Expand from seed customers
Tools: Ocean.io, AI Ark (option), DiscoLike (option)
Flow:
- Lookalikes from seeds | 5 to 10 seed domains at standard similarity, headcount and country matching the segment, paginated to the end | with: Ocean.io, AI Ark | chips: similarity 0.79, headcount, country
- Google-sourced check | Indexed from the open web rather than LinkedIn, so its count is a better gauge of the true market | with: DiscoLike | tag: market gauge

- Run Ocean.io first with 5 to 10 seed domains per segment at standard similarity (about 0.79), headcount and country set to the segment. Paginate to the end; the first page is not the market. Drop to 0.70 only when results are thin.
- AI Ark is an alternative when the user has it. DiscoLike sources from Google rather than LinkedIn, so when its count sits far above the database count, the database is under-reporting that segment.
- Keep the similarity score as a column for tiering later.

### 4. Pull where databases cannot see
Tools: Google Maps, Serper, Apify, Firecrawl
Flow:
- Google Maps and directories | Exact category by city for local or owner-operated segments, counted before anything is paid for | with: Google Maps, Apify | chips: category, city, phone, website
- Registries and ATS feeds | Licensing registries, association member lists and public job feeds, pulled broad and filtered later | with: Serper, Firecrawl | tag: free source

- If a segment is local or owner-operated, query Google Maps by exact category and city and scrape the results. Zip code beats city where categories are dense. The Local TAM from Google Maps play has the full method.
- Where the ICP is defined by a licence, a membership or a hiring pattern, go to the record itself: state licensing registries, association member lists, public ATS job feeds (Greenhouse, Lever and Ashby publish JSON). Serper finds the pages, Firecrawl or Apify fetch them. Pull broad, filter in step 6.
- Rows that arrive without a domain get one in a separate pass (company name plus city into Serper, first non-directory result), since asking inline costs recall.

### 5. Merge and dedupe
Tools: CSV, Google Sheets, Claude
Flow:
= One company, one row | Dedupe by domain, then company name plus state, then phone; keep every source that saw the company | with: CSV, Claude | chips: domain, name plus state, phone, source count | tag: floor not ceiling

- Normalize domains (strip protocol, www and paths) and merge on domain first. Match the remaining rows on normalized company name plus state, then on phone.
- When sources disagree on headcount or industry, keep both values in their own columns.
- Write a `sources` column listing every source that saw the company, and a `source_count`.
- Keep the raw pull from every source as its own file; the merged table is derived. Apply core: lists.
- Read the overlap. Under 30 percent means a third source is worth the pull. Any single source's count is a floor.

### 6. Qualify on the website, detect the stack
Tools: Firecrawl, Claude, BuiltWith (option)
Flow:
- Website read | Homepage plus about page, a yes or no ICP verdict with one reason from a cheap model, before any paid people lookup | with: Firecrawl, Claude | chips: verdict, reason, confidence | tag: cheap model
- Tech detect | Saved homepage HTML searched for the vendors the ICP requires or excludes | with: Claude, BuiltWith | chips: tech found, competitor present | tag: when ICP needs it

- Fetch the homepage and about page of every merged company. Have a cheap model answer: does this company match the ICP, yes or no, one reason, and a confidence. This routinely removes 25 to 40 percent of a raw pull.
- Keep the no's in the file with their verdict, out of the TAM count. A later ICP revision may want them.
- If the ICP names a technology (an ecommerce platform, a CRM, a competitor), search the saved homepage HTML for its script and link patterns; BuiltWith is the paid option when the pattern is not visible.
- Retry blocked and timed-out sites through a headless fetcher before marking them unknown; unknown stays in the TAM, flagged.
- Apply core: exclusions, so customers, open deals and competitors leave before anything is sized.

### 7. Size, write and schedule the refresh
Tools: Claude, Attio, HubSpot (option), Google Sheets (option)
Flow:
- Size the market | Qualified companies times buyers per company; over 100K contacts test broadly, under 30K go surgical | with: Claude | chips: companies, contacts, 60 to 80 pct reachable
= Write the TAM | Every company to the CRM or sheet with segment, source, verdict and tech columns; raw pulls kept; refresh in 60 days | with: Attio, HubSpot, Google Sheets | chips: segment, sources, verdict, pulled_at | tag: refresh 60 days

- Multiply qualified companies by the buyer count from step 2 for a contact estimate per segment. Above 100K contacts, be aggressive and test broadly. Below 30K, be surgical: every email matters.
- Expect to reach 60 to 80 percent of companies after enrichment. Plan campaign volume on that number, not on the TAM.
- Write every company to the CRM (or a sheet) with columns: segment, sources, source_count, icp_verdict, reason, tech_detected, similarity, pulled_at.
- Refresh in 60 days: re-run steps 2 to 4, merge new companies in, and flag those that no longer appear in any source.
- Print the count per segment with the sources that fed it. Apply core: credits and handoff.

## Guardrails

- Never report one source's count as the market. Say "at least" until a second source has been merged.
- Never spend a people-lookup credit on a company that has not passed the website read.
- Never let a vendor industry label include or exclude a company on its own. The website decides.
- A company without a domain is one to resolve, not one to drop.
- This play ends at a company list. Contacts, enrichment and sending belong to the plays built on it.
- A TAM older than 60 days is an estimate, not a map.

## Example run

User: "/pipeline-full-tam-map, ICP is in ./icp.md: payroll and compliance software for US staffing agencies, 20 to 500 people"

1. Read icp.md and split into two segments (light industrial staffing, healthcare staffing). Took 8 seed domains, confirmed Apollo, Ocean.io, DiscoLike and Attio.
2. Apollo returned 6,400 companies on industry filters; the 24-title people pass added 1,900 more and a buyer count averaging 2.8 per company.
3. Ocean.io returned 3,100 lookalikes at 0.79 across both segments. DiscoLike returned 4,700, which marked the database pull as a floor.
4. Two association member lists added 900 rows, 740 after domain resolution.
5. Merged to 12,300 unique domains, 11,800 after the name plus state and phone passes. Source overlap was 41 percent.
6. 7,900 passed the website read (67 percent). 212 removed as customers, open deals or competitors. Tech detect not needed for this ICP.
7. 7,688 companies, about 21,500 contacts, under 30K so surgical. Written to Attio with source columns, raw pulls kept, refresh set for 60 days.
Run this play with usPipeline.tech runs Full-TAM Map end to end for clients: infrastructure, lists, copy, sends and replies.

Play 03 · Foundation

Lookalike Sub-Verticals

Trigger Best customers as seeds
Outcome One campaign per sub-vertical, losers cut, winners scaled
Channels email, linkedin

Give this to your agent 
Install the Pipeline.tech "Lookalike Sub-Verticals" outbound play. Fetch
https://raw.githubusercontent.com/pipeline-tech/plays/main/core/SKILL.md and
https://raw.githubusercontent.com/pipeline-tech/plays/main/plays/lookalike-sub-verticals/SKILL.md,
save them as ~/.claude/skills/pipeline-outbound-core/SKILL.md and
~/.claude/skills/pipeline-lookalike-sub-verticals/SKILL.md, then run /pipeline-lookalike-sub-verticals.
Paste into Claude Code. It installs the shared core rules plus this play, then runs it as /pipeline-lookalike-sub-verticals.
Workflow, generated from the skillOpen full size

One broad ICP campaign averages every pain into one message and one number. Your best customers already show which sub-verticals buy, so expand from them, split the result into lanes, and give each lane its own copy and its own read. A proxy and web-data client went from about 1 lead per 2,290 emails on a broad send to about 1 per 283 on targeted sub-vertical lanes.

Load pipeline-outbound-core first. Where a step says "apply core", follow that section of the core skill.

Inputs

Ask for these before step 1, and save them to ./pipeline-plays.config.json:

  1. Five to ten seed domains: the customers the user would clone, picked by revenue, retention and ease of close, not logo size. Strip https://, www. and trailing slashes.
  2. The ICP in one paragraph: headcount band, geography, the problem you solve, who owns it, and what disqualifies an account.
  3. Stack answers from core section 1. Lookalike tool: Ocean.io first, AI Ark or DiscoLike if the user already has one.
  4. The offer in one sentence and one proof point per sub-vertical where the user has them.

Steps

1Expand from the seeds

ToolsOcean.ioAI Ark (option)DiscoLike (option)
  • Run the lookalike search three times with the same seeds and filters, once at each similarity floor: strict (0.85 and above), standard (0.79), broad (0.70). Keep the three pulls as separate lists with a similarity_band column. Each band is measured on its own in step 8.
  • Filter on the user's headcount band and countries only. No industry filters; the sort happens in step 2 from the website, not vendor labels.
  • Paginate until the pull is exhausted. A band under 50 companies: widen headcount before lowering the floor.
  • Apply core: lists (second source, dedupe by domain, source column, keep the raw pull).

2Sort into sub-verticals

ToolsSerperClaude
  • Fetch each company's homepage and one product page. Write a two-sentence summary: what they sell, to whom.
  • Ask the user to name 5 to 8 sub-verticals. Examples from the proof run, for a proxy and web-data offer: retail analytics, social media analytics, brand protection, ticketing, travel, SEO, web data extraction. If the user cannot name them, cluster the summaries with the model and propose labels for approval.
  • Label every row with exactly one sub-vertical, or other. If other passes 25 percent of a band, the seeds are too mixed; ask the user to split them.
  • Name one adjacent vertical outside the ICP that shares the underlying need (agencies for a data vendor, integrators for a platform). It gets its own list and campaign in step 7.

3Qualify before anything paid

ToolsClaude
  • Score every account 0 to 100 against the written ICP from the website summary, with one line of reasoning. Default pass mark 60. Apply core: lists (qualify before any paid lookup).
  • Expect 50 to 70 percent to pass. Under 40 means the seeds and the ICP paragraph disagree; stop and show the user the top disqualification reasons.
  • Apply core: exclusions.

4Find decision makers with title keywords

ToolsOcean.ioApolloLinkedIn
  • Pick the buyer roles by company size. Apply core: enrichment (decision maker by size).
  • For each role, generate 20 to 30 title keyword variations with the model: canonical, informal, legacy and regional forms ("data engineering", "data platform", "data infrastructure", "VP data", "director of data"). Search people by those keywords, not by seniority filters. Seniority filters miss the messy real titles, and that is where the volume is.
  • Target 3 to 5 contacts per company: the owner of the problem, the person above them, one or two peers who feel the pain. Under 50 people, one or two.
  • Apply core: enrichment (waterfall) and core: verification.

5Build one campaign per sub-vertical

ToolsEmailBisonInstantly (option)HeyReach
  • One campaign per sub-vertical, standard band first. Strict and broad follow as their own campaigns in weeks 2 and 3, so every band reads separately against the same copy. Own inbox set per campaign. Apply core: send rules.
  • Name campaigns {PLAY} {SUBVERTICAL} {BAND} {MONTH} so weekly reporting can group by any of the four.
  • Tier 2 contacts also get a LinkedIn connection request, no note, the same week as email one.

6Write pain-specific copy

ToolsClaude
  • Apply core: copy and send rules.
  • One pain per sub-vertical, in the words that sub-vertical uses. Ticketing gets a line about blocked checkouts during on-sales; brand protection gets marketplaces that geo-fence listings. Same offer, different first sentence, different proof point.
  • Draft 2 angle variants for email 1 per sub-vertical and one threaded nudge for email 2. Show the full set to the user before upload. A sub-vertical with no proof point runs the general angle and is flagged in the report.
  • Variables: {SUBVERTICAL_PAIN}, {PROOF}, {TITLE_SHORT}. No company-name personalization tricks; the lane is the personalization.

7Add the adjacent-vertical probe

ToolsEmailBisonInstantly (option)
  • Launch the adjacent vertical from step 2 as one more campaign at the standard band, same two-step structure, general angle. It is backup volume and a cheap test of a market the user has not considered.
  • If the probe lands under 1 lead per 500 emails over two weeks, read the replies for why, give it a sub-vertical label, and promote it to a full lane in step 5.

8Read lanes weekly, cut and scale

ToolsEmailBisonAttioSlack
  • Every Monday, per campaign: sent, replies, interested, emails per interested lead. Emails per lead decides; reply rate alone misleads.
  • Under 1 lead per 300 emails: scale. Add inboxes, pull the next similarity band, add adjacent titles.
  • Between 1:300 and 1:1,000: hold, rotate copy after 4 weeks.
  • Over 1 lead per 1,000 emails after 1,500 sends: cut the lane. Pause, log the reason, move the inboxes to a winner.
  • Do not judge a lane under 1,000 sends. Low volume reads as noise in both directions.
  • Apply core: CRM and alerts. Apply core: credits and handoff.

Guardrails

  • Never merge the three similarity bands into one list. Each band and each sub-vertical is measured by itself.
  • Never buy contacts for an account that has not passed the website ICP check. People lookups are the expensive step; the model read costs cents.
  • Never write the sub-vertical label into the email as a category ("as a ticketing company"). Write the pain it stands for.
  • Never cut a lane on reply rate. Cut on emails per lead, after the send floor.

Example run

User: "/pipeline-lookalike-sub-verticals, seeds are our 8 best customers, ICP is B2B software 100 to 2,000 people in the US and UK, we sell residential proxies"

  1. Confirmed seeds and ICP paragraph, Ocean.io plus EmailBison plus HeyReach plus Attio.
  2. Three pulls: 410 strict, 1,180 standard, 2,640 broad. 3,560 unique companies after dedupe, each with its band label.
  3. Website summaries sorted into 7 sub-verticals plus other (14 percent). Adjacent vertical: marketing agencies.
  4. 2,210 passed the ICP check at 60, 88 removed as customers, open deals or recent sequences.
  5. 24 title variations per role, 7,340 contacts found, 5,910 verified valid, catch-alls in their own campaigns.
  6. 7 standard-band campaigns plus one agency probe launched across 16 inboxes after copy approval; strict and broad bands queued for weeks 2 and 3.
  7. Week 4 read: web data extraction at 1:240 and brand protection at 1:310 scaled; travel at 1:1,900 cut; agency probe at 1:470 flagged for promotion.
The skill fileplays/lookalike-sub-verticals/SKILL.mdExactly what gets installed. Copy it, save it as ~/.claude/skills/pipeline-lookalike-sub-verticals/SKILL.md next to the core skill, and run /pipeline-lookalike-sub-verticals.

# Lookalike Sub-Verticals

## Example run

User: "/pipeline-lookalike-sub-verticals, seeds are our 8 best customers, ICP is B2B software 100 to 2,000 people in the US and UK, we sell residential proxies"

6. 7 standard-band campaigns plus one agency probe launched across 16 inboxes after copy approval; strict and broad bands queued for weeks 2 and 3.
7. Week 4 read: web data extraction at 1:240 and brand protection at 1:310 scaled; travel at 1:1,900 cut; agency probe at 1:470 flagged for promotion.
Run this play with usPipeline.tech runs Lookalike Sub-Verticals end to end for clients: infrastructure, lists, copy, sends and replies.

Play 04 · Foundation

Pain-Qualified Segments

Trigger Buyer calls, won-deal context and public fields
Outcome Segments written as situation plus pain, each with its own opener
Channels email, linkedin

Give this to your agent 
Install the Pipeline.tech "Pain-Qualified Segments" outbound play. Fetch
https://raw.githubusercontent.com/pipeline-tech/plays/main/core/SKILL.md and
https://raw.githubusercontent.com/pipeline-tech/plays/main/plays/pain-qualified-segments/SKILL.md,
save them as ~/.claude/skills/pipeline-outbound-core/SKILL.md and
~/.claude/skills/pipeline-pain-qualified-segments/SKILL.md, then run /pipeline-pain-qualified-segments.
Paste into Claude Code. It installs the shared core rules plus this play, then runs it as /pipeline-pain-qualified-segments.
Workflow, generated from the skillOpen full size

An ICP tells you which companies to contact, not why any of them would answer. A Pain-Qualified Segment (PQS), the Pipeline.tech framework for this, is a group of personas who share one specific pain your offer fixes, described in the words buyers used on calls. It is the bridge between ICP research and copy, and it decides what each campaign opens on.

Load pipeline-outbound-core first. Where a step says "apply core", follow that section of the core skill.

Inputs

Ask for these before step 1, and save them to ./pipeline-plays.config.json:

  1. The ICP in one paragraph, or the ICP file if one exists. This play names why those companies buy.
  2. Buyer-side call transcripts from the last 12 months: discovery calls, first calls, demos where the buyer did most of the talking. Under 15 calls, every segment carries the hypothesis label until more come in.
  3. Won and lost deals from the CRM with close reason, plus support tickets if they exist.
  4. Stack answers from core section 1.
  5. The offer in one sentence. A pain the offer does not fix is never a segment.

Steps

1Gather the raw material

ToolsFireflies (option)Gong (option)AttioHubSpotGoogle Sheets
  • Pull every buyer-side transcript from the last 12 months. Internal calls and vendor-led demos carry little; the buyer has to be the one talking.
  • Pull won and lost deals with close reason, deal size, days to close and what they replaced. Support tickets carry the operational layer word for word.
  • Keep one row per document with source, account, persona and stage so every phrase can be traced to its call.

2Mine each call for the buying situation

ToolsClaudeGoogle Sheets
  • Run every transcript through the same four questions: what was true in the business when they took the call, why now rather than last quarter, what they had already tried and why it stopped working, and the pain in their own words.
  • Copy the phrases verbatim into a language bank, tagged by persona, account and deal outcome. A paraphrase loses the words that make an opener sound like a colleague wrote it.
  • Read lost deals as carefully as won ones. A pain the offer did not fix is a segment you exclude on purpose.

3Sort pains into four layers

ToolsClaudeGoogle Sheets
  • Tag every phrase with one layer: operational (the daily workflow), strategic (the business outcome), personal (the persona's own career or standing) or financial (budget and return).
  • Personal is the layer most teams skip and the one that makes copy land. A persona with no personal entry sends you back to the transcripts.
  • Count phrases per layer per persona. A pain named by three or more buyers across different accounts is a candidate; a pain named once is an anecdote.

4Write the segments

ToolsClaudeGoogle Sheets
  • Group personas by shared pain, not by title. A VP and a manager naming the same pain in the same situation share a segment; two VPs with different pains do not.
  • Write each segment with seven fields: name, the situation (what is true in the business), the pain in the buyers' words, the trigger that makes it urgent now, the impact in numbers (hours, money, headcount, missed targets), solution urgency (how long they can live with it) and a one-line message angle.
  • Kill vague segments ("wants to grow") and single-signal segments (a title with no situation behind it). A segment that needs two messages is split.
  • Take impact numbers from transcripts or the CRM. A pain without a number is an assertion.

5Write the membership test on public data

ToolsApolloOcean.ioAI ArkSerperExaClaude
  • Turn each situation into a membership test: one line per condition with source, field, operator and threshold, such as sales headcount in Apollo greater than 8.
  • Mark each condition public (readable in a database), proxy (inferred, such as a job post standing in for a hiring plan, with the proxy named) or not public.
  • Not-public conditions cannot filter a list. Move them into the first-call questions for that segment, so the opener asks what the data cannot see.
  • A segment with no public or proxy condition is a hypothesis, not a list. Keep it, label it, do not build it yet.

6Measure before building

ToolsApolloOcean.ioAI ArkAttioGoogle Sheets
  • Count every segment with a free query before exporting anything. Under 300 companies is a hand campaign; above 20K the test is too loose.
  • Backtest: pull your customers as they looked 90 days before they bought (headcount, hires, stack at the time) and run the test on them, then on a random sample of ICP companies. Lift under 2x means the situation is not the one that made them buy.
  • Label each segment measured (counted and backtested), partly measured (counted only) or hypothesis (neither). Build measured segments first.

7Build the list and the opener per segment

ToolsApolloOcean.ioClaudeEmailBison
  • Build one list per segment from its membership test. Apply core: lists, exclusions, enrichment and verification.
  • Write the opener per segment like a technician asking a diagnostic question: open on the situation in the buyers' words, say what you saw and where (the public or proxy condition), and ask whether the read is right. Pull language from that segment's part of the bank only.
  • Apply core: copy and send rules. One segment per campaign, never mixed. These segments and openers become the industry and persona cells of the Full-TAM Cold Email play.

8Hand off and keep the bank alive

ToolsAttioEmailBisonGoogle Sheets
  • Write the segment name and label on every contact in the CRM, so reply and interested rates read per segment.
  • Every new buyer call appends to the bank. Revisit the segments each quarter; a hypothesis segment that outperforms the measured ones is the next to count and backtest.
  • Apply core: credits and handoff.

Guardrails

  • Never quote one buyer to another. Use the language, never the attribution, never a sentence a prospect could trace to a named company.
  • A pain the offer does not fix is not a segment, however often it was named.
  • Never mix two segments in one campaign to reach a volume floor. If two segments share one message they were one segment all along.
  • Inferred situations stay hypotheses in copy. "Looks like" and "if I read this right" are the register; an inference stated as fact turns a diagnostic opener into a guess.
  • Every impact number not traceable to a transcript or the CRM is marked as an estimate.

Example run

User: "/pipeline-pain-qualified-segments for our revenue operations platform, ICP is B2B SaaS 100 to 1K people in North America"

  1. Confirmed Attio, Apollo and EmailBison. Pulled 64 buyer-side transcripts, 38 won and 41 lost deals with close reasons, 212 support tickets.
  2. Mined 64 calls into 410 verbatim phrases across 6 personas.
  3. Layers: 171 operational, 94 strategic, 58 personal, 87 financial. Three personas needed a second pass to fill the personal layer.
  4. 11 candidate segments; 4 killed as vague or single-signal, 1 split, 8 written with all seven fields.
  5. 31 membership conditions: 17 public, 9 proxy, 5 moved to first-call questions.
  6. Counts from 2.1K to 14K companies. Top segment backtested at 61% hit rate on customers against 12% on the market sample. 5 measured, 2 partly measured, 1 hypothesis.
  7. 5 measured lists built and verified, 5 openers approved from the language bank, loaded into one campaign each with segment and label written to Attio.

# Pain-Qualified Segments

An ICP tells you which companies to contact, not why any of them would answer. A Pain-Qualified Segment (PQS), the Pipeline.tech framework for this, is a group of personas who share one specific pain your offer fixes, described in the words buyers used on calls. It is the bridge between ICP research and copy, and it decides what each campaign opens on.

Load `pipeline-outbound-core` first. Where a step says "apply core", follow that section of the core skill.

## Inputs

Ask for these before step 1, and save them to `./pipeline-plays.config.json`:

1. The ICP in one paragraph, or the ICP file if one exists. This play names why those companies buy.
2. Buyer-side call transcripts from the last 12 months: discovery calls, first calls, demos where the buyer did most of the talking. Under 15 calls, every segment carries the hypothesis label until more come in.
3. Won and lost deals from the CRM with close reason, plus support tickets if they exist.
4. Stack answers from core section 1.
5. The offer in one sentence. A pain the offer does not fix is never a segment.

## Steps

### 1. Gather the raw material
Tools: Fireflies (option), Gong (option), Attio, HubSpot, Google Sheets
Flow:
- Buyer-side calls | Discovery and first calls from the last 12 months, transcripts exported in full | with: Fireflies, Gong | chips: date, account, persona, outcome
- Won and lost deals | Close reason, deal size, days to close, what they replaced | with: Attio, HubSpot | chips: won, lost, reason
= One row per document | Source, account, persona and stage, so every quote stays traceable | with: Google Sheets | tag: traceable

- Pull every buyer-side transcript from the last 12 months. Internal calls and vendor-led demos carry little; the buyer has to be the one talking.
- Pull won and lost deals with close reason, deal size, days to close and what they replaced. Support tickets carry the operational layer word for word.
- Keep one row per document with source, account, persona and stage so every phrase can be traced to its call.

### 2. Mine each call for the buying situation
Tools: Claude, Google Sheets
Flow:
- Four questions per call | What was true when they decided to talk, why now, what they had already tried, the pain in their own words | with: Claude | chips: situation, why now, tried, quote
= Language bank | Verbatim phrases tagged by persona, account and outcome, never paraphrased | with: Google Sheets | tag: verbatim only

- Run every transcript through the same four questions: what was true in the business when they took the call, why now rather than last quarter, what they had already tried and why it stopped working, and the pain in their own words.
- Copy the phrases verbatim into a language bank, tagged by persona, account and deal outcome. A paraphrase loses the words that make an opener sound like a colleague wrote it.
- Read lost deals as carefully as won ones. A pain the offer did not fix is a segment you exclude on purpose.

### 3. Sort pains into four layers
Tools: Claude, Google Sheets
Flow:
- Operational | Day-to-day workflow pain | chips: hours, errors, handoffs
- Strategic | Business outcome and growth pain | chips: targets, markets, competitors
- Personal | Career and reputation pain for the persona | chips: board, boss, credibility
= Financial | Budget and return pain, always with the number attached | chips: spend, waste, payback | tag: three or more buyers

- Tag every phrase with one layer: operational (the daily workflow), strategic (the business outcome), personal (the persona's own career or standing) or financial (budget and return).
- Personal is the layer most teams skip and the one that makes copy land. A persona with no personal entry sends you back to the transcripts.
- Count phrases per layer per persona. A pain named by three or more buyers across different accounts is a candidate; a pain named once is an anecdote.

### 4. Write the segments
Tools: Claude, Google Sheets
Flow:
- Seven fields per segment | Name, situation, pain, trigger, impact in numbers, solution urgency, one-line angle | with: Claude | chips: situation, pain, trigger, impact, urgency, angle
= Kill or split | Vague and single-signal segments are cut; a segment that needs two messages is two segments | tag: one segment one message

- Group personas by shared pain, not by title. A VP and a manager naming the same pain in the same situation share a segment; two VPs with different pains do not.
- Write each segment with seven fields: name, the situation (what is true in the business), the pain in the buyers' words, the trigger that makes it urgent now, the impact in numbers (hours, money, headcount, missed targets), solution urgency (how long they can live with it) and a one-line message angle.
- Kill vague segments ("wants to grow") and single-signal segments (a title with no situation behind it). A segment that needs two messages is split.
- Take impact numbers from transcripts or the CRM. A pain without a number is an assertion.

### 5. Write the membership test on public data
Tools: Apollo, Ocean.io, AI Ark, Serper, Exa, Claude
Flow:
- Field by field | Every condition in the situation gets a source, a field, an operator and a threshold | with: Apollo, Ocean.io, AI Ark | chips: source, field, operator, threshold
- Public, proxy or not public | Each condition marked for how it can be read from outside | with: Serper, Exa, Claude | tag: honest labels
= First-call questions | Not-public conditions leave the filter and become the first thing you ask | chips: question, segment

- Turn each situation into a membership test: one line per condition with source, field, operator and threshold, such as sales headcount in Apollo greater than 8.
- Mark each condition public (readable in a database), proxy (inferred, such as a job post standing in for a hiring plan, with the proxy named) or not public.
- Not-public conditions cannot filter a list. Move them into the first-call questions for that segment, so the opener asks what the data cannot see.
- A segment with no public or proxy condition is a hypothesis, not a list. Keep it, label it, do not build it yet.

### 6. Measure before building
Tools: Apollo, Ocean.io, AI Ark, Attio, Google Sheets
Flow:
- Count the segment | The membership test run as a free database query before any export | with: Apollo, Ocean.io, AI Ark | chips: companies, contacts
- Backtest on customers | Did the test match your customers 90 days before they bought, against a market sample | with: Attio | chips: customer hit rate, market hit rate, lift
= Label every segment | Measured, partly measured or hypothesis, written on the segment row | with: Google Sheets | tag: no unlabelled segments

- Count every segment with a free query before exporting anything. Under 300 companies is a hand campaign; above 20K the test is too loose.
- Backtest: pull your customers as they looked 90 days before they bought (headcount, hires, stack at the time) and run the test on them, then on a random sample of ICP companies. Lift under 2x means the situation is not the one that made them buy.
- Label each segment measured (counted and backtested), partly measured (counted only) or hypothesis (neither). Build measured segments first.

### 7. Build the list and the opener per segment
Tools: Apollo, Ocean.io, Claude, EmailBison
Flow:
- One list per segment | The membership test is the filter; exclusions, enrichment and verification follow core | with: Apollo, Ocean.io | tag: core: lists
= Diagnostic opener | The situation in the buyers' words, what you saw and where, then ask if the read is right | with: Claude, EmailBison | chips: {SITUATION}, {SEEN_WHERE}, one segment per campaign

- Build one list per segment from its membership test. Apply core: lists, exclusions, enrichment and verification.
- Write the opener per segment like a technician asking a diagnostic question: open on the situation in the buyers' words, say what you saw and where (the public or proxy condition), and ask whether the read is right. Pull language from that segment's part of the bank only.
- Apply core: copy and send rules. One segment per campaign, never mixed. These segments and openers become the industry and persona cells of the Full-TAM Cold Email play.

### 8. Hand off and keep the bank alive
Tools: Attio, EmailBison, Google Sheets
Flow:
= Segment as a CRM field | Every contact carries its segment and label; every new call appends to the bank; a hypothesis that replies gets measured next | with: Attio, EmailBison, Google Sheets | chips: segment, label, reply rate, interested rate

- Write the segment name and label on every contact in the CRM, so reply and interested rates read per segment.
- Every new buyer call appends to the bank. Revisit the segments each quarter; a hypothesis segment that outperforms the measured ones is the next to count and backtest.
- Apply core: credits and handoff.

## Guardrails

- Never quote one buyer to another. Use the language, never the attribution, never a sentence a prospect could trace to a named company.
- A pain the offer does not fix is not a segment, however often it was named.
- Never mix two segments in one campaign to reach a volume floor. If two segments share one message they were one segment all along.
- Inferred situations stay hypotheses in copy. "Looks like" and "if I read this right" are the register; an inference stated as fact turns a diagnostic opener into a guess.
- Every impact number not traceable to a transcript or the CRM is marked as an estimate.

## Example run

User: "/pipeline-pain-qualified-segments for our revenue operations platform, ICP is B2B SaaS 100 to 1K people in North America"

1. Confirmed Attio, Apollo and EmailBison. Pulled 64 buyer-side transcripts, 38 won and 41 lost deals with close reasons, 212 support tickets.
2. Mined 64 calls into 410 verbatim phrases across 6 personas.
3. Layers: 171 operational, 94 strategic, 58 personal, 87 financial. Three personas needed a second pass to fill the personal layer.
4. 11 candidate segments; 4 killed as vague or single-signal, 1 split, 8 written with all seven fields.
5. 31 membership conditions: 17 public, 9 proxy, 5 moved to first-call questions.
6. Counts from 2.1K to 14K companies. Top segment backtested at 61% hit rate on customers against 12% on the market sample. 5 measured, 2 partly measured, 1 hypothesis.
7. 5 measured lists built and verified, 5 openers approved from the language bank, loaded into one campaign each with segment and label written to Attio.

Play 05 · Foundation

Local TAM from Google Maps

Trigger Exact Google Maps categories by city
Outcome A local market at cents per lead, owners not info@
Channels email, call

Give this to your agent 
Install the Pipeline.tech "Local TAM from Google Maps" outbound play. Fetch
https://raw.githubusercontent.com/pipeline-tech/plays/main/core/SKILL.md and
https://raw.githubusercontent.com/pipeline-tech/plays/main/plays/local-tam-google-maps/SKILL.md,
save them as ~/.claude/skills/pipeline-outbound-core/SKILL.md and
~/.claude/skills/pipeline-local-tam-google-maps/SKILL.md, then run /pipeline-local-tam-google-maps.
Paste into Claude Code. It installs the shared core rules plus this play, then runs it as /pipeline-local-tam-google-maps.
Workflow, generated from the skillOpen full size

Coffee shops, plumbers and roofers are not in Apollo or ZoomInfo, and the owner is the buyer. Google Maps lists every one of them with a phone, a website and an exact category. Query the exact category city by city, count before you pay, then spend the money on finding the owner. Pipeline.tech runs this at about 0.03 USD per lead; the same lead costs about 1.22 USD in a no-code enrichment tool.

Load pipeline-outbound-core first. Where a step says "apply core", follow that section of the core skill.

Inputs

Ask for these before step 1, and save them to ./pipeline-plays.config.json:

  1. The offer in one sentence, one proof point, and the free asset you can make for one business (a branded mockup, a short audit, a list of nearby buyers). The asset is the CTA.
  2. The verticals in plain words ("coffee shops", "plumbers") and the geography.
  3. Which categories are seasonal. Ice cream has a window; roofers do not.
  4. Stack answers from core section 1. Default scraper is Serper's Maps endpoint; Outscraper or Apify if the user has one.

Steps

1Pick exact categories per vertical

ToolsGoogle MapsClaude
  • Translate each vertical into the exact category strings Maps uses. "Coffee shops" becomes Coffee shop, Coffee roasters, Espresso bar, Cafe. Never query a generic parent such as Restaurant or Contractor: a promotional products client saw coffee shops reply at about 3x the rate of generic restaurants on the same offer.
  • Write the anti-ICP list before scraping: chains and franchises, fine dining, hotel restaurants, hospital, school and corporate cafeterias, anything institutional. These buy through procurement.

2Build the grid and size the market

ToolsSerperGoogle Sheets
  • Build a query file with one category|city, ST per line: the top 20 to 50 cities by population plus the suburbs of each major metro. Skip small towns.
  • Run one page per query and record the result count. Sum by category and by city. That is the market size, and it costs cents. Prune categories under roughly 10 results per city. Show the counts to the user before paying for enrichment.
  • Expect about 15 unique businesses per query. An internal run across Texas trades turned 205 queries into about 3,000 businesses.

3Scrape and dedupe

ToolsSerperOutscraper (option)Apify (option)
  • Serper's Maps endpoint is POST https://google.serper.dev/maps with body {"q": "<category> <city, ST>", "num": 100}.
  • Pull every query. Keep name, category, street address, phone, website domain, review count, place id.
  • Apply core: lists, with one local change. Dedupe by domain first, then by business name plus street address, since one trade name repeats across towns.
  • Write the query that found each row in a source column. Keep the raw pull.

4Qualify before you enrich

ToolsClaudeFirecrawl
  • Drop the anti-ICP categories. Drop chains: any name in more than three cities, any domain with a store locator, any site listing more than five locations.
  • Keep 5 to 50 employees only. 51 to 200 is procurement and owner copy stops working, 200 plus is corporate purchasing. Estimate headcount from the website, review volume and location count.
  • Apply core: exclusions.
  • Rows with no website stay flagged no-site for the call lane.

5Resolve the owner name

ToolsBlitzAPISerperClaudeLinkedIn
  • Run a five-layer waterfall and stop at the first named person: LinkedIn company page owner or founder; the website's about or team page read by an LLM; a Google search for "<business name>" owner; the Facebook business page; the state business registry. Each layer only sees the rows the one before it missed.
  • Keep a confidence score and the source layer on every row; flag registry hits, they are weaker.
  • Expect about 80% of businesses to return an owner name. The rest feed the public-inbox and call lanes.

6Find the owner email

ToolsBlitzAPIProspeoIcyPeasFirecrawl
  • Apply core: enrichment. For owner-operators run BlitzAPI first (strongest on small-business addresses), then Prospeo, then IcyPeas.
  • Rows with no owner email get a public inbox scraped from the website (info@, contact@, hello@) in a public_email column flagged lane: public. Never pattern-guess a role address; only use what the site publishes.
  • Expect about 85% of the list to have some email, roughly a third of it an owner address. Report the split; the two lanes convert differently.

7Verify and segment

ToolsMillionVerifierBounceBanGoogle Sheets
  • Apply core: verification. Public inboxes go through both verifiers too.
  • Segment by category, not by city. One campaign and one copy set per category.
  • Add merge fields per segment: {TRADE_NOUN} ("plumber"), {TRADE_PLURAL} ("plumbing companies"), {TRADE_WORK} ("plumbing"), plus {CATEGORY} and {CITY}. Fallbacks: first name "there" when no owner was found, last name "-" if the sender requires one.

8Write and launch, two lanes

ToolsEmailBisonInstantly (option)Dialer
  • Apply core: copy and send rules.
  • Angle: open on something true of the category's daily work, not of the business ("property managers in {CITY} keep looking for a reliable {TRADE_NOUN}"). Email 1 offers the free made-for-them asset, never a call. Email 2 is the threaded "are you the right person for this" bump.
  • Lane one: owner emails, main inbox set. Lane two: public inboxes, its own campaign and inbox set, same copy with the "there" fallback. Never mix them; their numbers must read apart. The no-site and no-email rows go to a call list.
  • Respect the window: ice cream and food trucks in February to April, breweries in March to April and August, bakeries in September to October and January, catering in January and September.
  • Draft 3 angle variants per category and show them for approval before upload.

9Report

ToolsEmailBisonAttioSlack
  • Weekly, per category and per lane: scraped, qualified, owner found, owner email, public inbox, sent, reply rate, interested, meetings, cost per lead. Retire a category under half the best one's reply rate after 1,000 sends; scale the best into the next geography.
  • Apply core: credits and handoff.

Guardrails

  • The public-inbox lane is a real conflict in our data. One source saw 2 to 4% reply and 10 to 15% positive on info@ style addresses. Another offer saw role emails fail to convert at all. Run it as its own lane, read it on its own, and let the user's numbers settle it.
  • Never query a generic category to "cover more". It costs the same to scrape and about 3x more per reply.
  • Never upload a registry-layer owner name without the flag; a registered agent is not the owner.
  • One business, one sequence. If both the owner and the public inbox resolve, the owner gets the email and the public inbox waits for a later rotation.

Example run

User: "/pipeline-local-tam-google-maps for our commercial-client outreach offer, trades in Texas, free asset is a list of 20 property managers near them"

  1. Confirmed the offer line, chose Plumber, HVAC contractor, Electrician, Roofing contractor and General contractor, 41 cities and suburbs, 205 queries.
  2. Sized the grid at one page per query, about 3,100 results, two small cities pruned.
  3. Scraped the full grid: 3,006 unique businesses after dedupe, 212 chain locations removed, 2,640 kept inside 5 to 50 employees.
  4. Owner name found for about 80%. Owner email verified for about a third, public inbox for most of the rest, 85% with some address; 310 no-site rows to the call list.
  5. Five campaigns per lane ("TX Plumbers owners", "TX Plumbers public"), 3 angles approved, scheduled for Monday, about 0.03 USD per lead all in.

USE WHEN user says "/pipeline-local-tam-google-maps" OR "local TAM play" OR "scrape Google Maps for leads" OR "local business list" OR "owner-operator outreach" OR "Maps categories by city".
