# Local TAM from Google Maps

Coffee shops, plumbers and roofers are not in Apollo or ZoomInfo, and the owner is the buyer. Google Maps lists every one of them with a phone, a website and an exact category. Query the exact category city by city, count before you pay, then spend the money on finding the owner. Pipeline.tech runs this at about 0.03 USD per lead; the same lead costs about 1.22 USD in a no-code enrichment tool.

Load `pipeline-outbound-core` first. Where a step says "apply core", follow that section of the core skill.

## Inputs

Ask for these before step 1, and save them to `./pipeline-plays.config.json`:

1. The offer in one sentence, one proof point, and the free asset you can make for one business (a branded mockup, a short audit, a list of nearby buyers). The asset is the CTA.
2. The verticals in plain words ("coffee shops", "plumbers") and the geography.
3. Which categories are seasonal. Ice cream has a window; roofers do not.
4. Stack answers from core section 1. Default scraper is Serper's Maps endpoint; Outscraper or Apify if the user has one.

## Steps

### 1. Pick exact categories per vertical
Tools: Google Maps, Claude
Flow:
- Exact category strings | Coffee shop, Coffee roasters, Espresso bar, Cafe; never Restaurant or Contractor | with: Google Maps | tag: 3x the replies
- Anti-ICP list | Chains, franchises, fine dining, hotel, hospital, school and corporate cafeterias | with: Claude | tag: procurement buys

- Translate each vertical into the exact category strings Maps uses. "Coffee shops" becomes Coffee shop, Coffee roasters, Espresso bar, Cafe. Never query a generic parent such as Restaurant or Contractor: a promotional products client saw coffee shops reply at about 3x the rate of generic restaurants on the same offer.
- Write the anti-ICP list before scraping: chains and franchises, fine dining, hotel restaurants, hospital, school and corporate cafeterias, anything institutional. These buy through procurement.

### 2. Build the grid and size the market
Tools: Serper, Google Sheets
Flow:
= One category-by-city line per query, one page each, counts summed | Top 20 to 50 cities plus metro suburbs; prune under 10 results per city; show counts before paying | with: Serper, Google Sheets | chips: about 15 businesses per query, 205 queries to about 3K

- Build a query file with one `category|city, ST` per line: the top 20 to 50 cities by population plus the suburbs of each major metro. Skip small towns.
- Run one page per query and record the result count. Sum by category and by city. That is the market size, and it costs cents. Prune categories under roughly 10 results per city. Show the counts to the user before paying for enrichment.
- Expect about 15 unique businesses per query. An internal run across Texas trades turned 205 queries into about 3,000 businesses.

### 3. Scrape and dedupe
Tools: Serper, Outscraper (option), Apify (option)
Flow:
= Every query pulled, deduped by domain then name plus street | with: Serper, Outscraper, Apify | chips: name, category, address, phone, domain, reviews, place id, source

- Serper's Maps endpoint is `POST https://google.serper.dev/maps` with body `{"q": "<category> <city, ST>", "num": 100}`.
- Pull every query. Keep name, category, street address, phone, website domain, review count, place id.
- Apply core: lists, with one local change. Dedupe by domain first, then by business name plus street address, since one trade name repeats across towns.
- Write the query that found each row in a `source` column. Keep the raw pull.

### 4. Qualify before you enrich
Tools: Claude, Firecrawl
Flow:
= Drop chains and anti-ICP, keep 5 to 50 employees | 51 to 200 is procurement, 200 plus is corporate purchasing | with: Claude, Firecrawl | chips: store locator, name in 3+ cities, 5+ locations, no-site flag

- Drop the anti-ICP categories. Drop chains: any name in more than three cities, any domain with a store locator, any site listing more than five locations.
- Keep 5 to 50 employees only. 51 to 200 is procurement and owner copy stops working, 200 plus is corporate purchasing. Estimate headcount from the website, review volume and location count.
- Apply core: exclusions.
- Rows with no website stay flagged `no-site` for the call lane.

### 5. Resolve the owner name
Tools: BlitzAPI, Serper, Claude, LinkedIn
Flow:
- LinkedIn page | with: LinkedIn | tag: layer 1
- Website read | with: Claude, Firecrawl | tag: layer 2
- Google owner search | with: Serper | tag: layer 3
= Facebook, then state registry | Each layer only sees what the one before missed; registry hits flagged as weak | with: BlitzAPI | chips: owner name, confidence, source layer | tag: about 80% hit

- Run a five-layer waterfall and stop at the first named person: LinkedIn company page owner or founder; the website's about or team page read by an LLM; a Google search for `"<business name>" owner`; the Facebook business page; the state business registry. Each layer only sees the rows the one before it missed.
- Keep a confidence score and the source layer on every row; flag registry hits, they are weaker.
- Expect about 80% of businesses to return an owner name. The rest feed the public-inbox and call lanes.

### 6. Find the owner email
Tools: BlitzAPI, Prospeo, IcyPeas, Firecrawl
Flow:
- Owner email waterfall | Small-business addresses first | with: BlitzAPI, Prospeo, IcyPeas | tag: core: enrichment
- Public inbox fallback | info@, contact@, hello@ scraped from the site, never guessed, flagged as its own lane | with: Firecrawl | tag: lane: public

- Apply core: enrichment. For owner-operators run BlitzAPI first (strongest on small-business addresses), then Prospeo, then IcyPeas.
- Rows with no owner email get a public inbox scraped from the website (info@, contact@, hello@) in a `public_email` column flagged `lane: public`. Never pattern-guess a role address; only use what the site publishes.
- Expect about 85% of the list to have some email, roughly a third of it an owner address. Report the split; the two lanes convert differently.

### 7. Verify and segment
Tools: MillionVerifier, BounceBan, Google Sheets
Flow:
= Two passes, then one campaign per category | Segment by category, not city | with: MillionVerifier, BounceBan, Google Sheets | chips: {TRADE_NOUN}, {TRADE_PLURAL}, {TRADE_WORK}, {CATEGORY}, {CITY}

- Apply core: verification. Public inboxes go through both verifiers too.
- Segment by category, not by city. One campaign and one copy set per category.
- Add merge fields per segment: `{TRADE_NOUN}` ("plumber"), `{TRADE_PLURAL}` ("plumbing companies"), `{TRADE_WORK}` ("plumbing"), plus `{CATEGORY}` and `{CITY}`. Fallbacks: first name "there" when no owner was found, last name "-" if the sender requires one.

### 8. Write and launch, two lanes
Tools: EmailBison, Instantly (option), Dialer
Flow:
- Owner lane | Main inbox set; opens on the category's daily work, offers the made-for-them asset | with: EmailBison, Instantly | tag: send
- Public inbox lane | Own campaign and inboxes, same copy with the there fallback | with: EmailBison | tag: read apart
- Call list | no-site and no-email rows | with: Dialer

- Apply core: copy and send rules.
- Angle: open on something true of the category's daily work, not of the business ("property managers in {CITY} keep looking for a reliable {TRADE_NOUN}"). Email 1 offers the free made-for-them asset, never a call. Email 2 is the threaded "are you the right person for this" bump.
- Lane one: owner emails, main inbox set. Lane two: public inboxes, its own campaign and inbox set, same copy with the "there" fallback. Never mix them; their numbers must read apart. The `no-site` and no-email rows go to a call list.
- Respect the window: ice cream and food trucks in February to April, breweries in March to April and August, bakeries in September to October and January, catering in January and September.
- Draft 3 angle variants per category and show them for approval before upload.

### 9. Report
Tools: EmailBison, Attio, Slack
Flow:
= Per category and per lane, weekly | Retire a category under half the best one's reply rate after 1,000 sends; scale the best into the next geography | with: EmailBison, Attio, Slack | chips: cost per lead

- Weekly, per category and per lane: scraped, qualified, owner found, owner email, public inbox, sent, reply rate, interested, meetings, cost per lead. Retire a category under half the best one's reply rate after 1,000 sends; scale the best into the next geography.
- Apply core: credits and handoff.

## Guardrails

- The public-inbox lane is a real conflict in our data. One source saw 2 to 4% reply and 10 to 15% positive on info@ style addresses. Another offer saw role emails fail to convert at all. Run it as its own lane, read it on its own, and let the user's numbers settle it.
- Never query a generic category to "cover more". It costs the same to scrape and about 3x more per reply.
- Never upload a registry-layer owner name without the flag; a registered agent is not the owner.
- One business, one sequence. If both the owner and the public inbox resolve, the owner gets the email and the public inbox waits for a later rotation.

## Example run

User: "/pipeline-local-tam-google-maps for our commercial-client outreach offer, trades in Texas, free asset is a list of 20 property managers near them"

1. Confirmed the offer line, chose Plumber, HVAC contractor, Electrician, Roofing contractor and General contractor, 41 cities and suburbs, 205 queries.
2. Sized the grid at one page per query, about 3,100 results, two small cities pruned.
3. Scraped the full grid: 3,006 unique businesses after dedupe, 212 chain locations removed, 2,640 kept inside 5 to 50 employees.
4. Owner name found for about 80%. Owner email verified for about a third, public inbox for most of the rest, 85% with some address; 310 no-site rows to the call list.
5. Five campaigns per lane ("TX Plumbers owners", "TX Plumbers public"), 3 angles approved, scheduled for Monday, about 0.03 USD per lead all in.

Org-Chart Qualifier

Outcome Qualify by who they employ, not by industry tag
Channels email, linkedin

Give this to your agent

https://raw.githubusercontent.com/pipeline-tech/plays/main/core/SKILL.md and
https://raw.githubusercontent.com/pipeline-tech/plays/main/plays/org-chart-qualifier/SKILL.md,
save them as ~/.claude/skills/pipeline-outbound-core/SKILL.md and

Paste into Claude Code. It installs the shared core rules plus this play, then runs it as /pipeline-org-chart-qualifier.
Workflow, generated from the skillOpen full size

Some needs show up as a job title before they show up anywhere else. A Director of Clinical Education means courses for clinicians, a Head of Partnerships means a channel, a RevOps Manager means a sales stack, a Community Manager means a community. Search for the title, collect the companies behind it, and the list is qualified by what each company actually does.

Load pipeline-outbound-core first. Where a step says "apply core", follow that section of the core skill.

Inputs

Ask for these before step 1, and save them to ./pipeline-plays.config.json:

  1. The ICP in one paragraph: headcount band, geography, the problem you solve, who owns it. If the user has an ICP file, read it.
  2. The offer in one sentence, one proof point, and the pain it removes. The tell-tale titles come from the pain, not the product.
  3. Stack answers from core section 1, plus which people database supports an organization-level title filter. Apollo is the default.
  4. A headcount cap; without one a title search returns the enterprise end of every market first.

Steps

1Name the tell-tale titles

ToolsClaude
  • With the user, list two to five titles whose existence proves the need, and write what each proves: "Director of Clinical Education" proves the company trains clinicians and runs courses. If a title could exist at a company that does not need you, drop it.
  • Expand each title into 20 to 30 keyword variations: canonical, the informal form people type on LinkedIn, the legacy name, regional spellings, the abbreviation. Volume hides in the messy variants, so "Clinical Educator" and "Clinical Ed" count.
  • Keep one row per variation with its parent title and what it proves; it is the filter for step 2 and the angle source for step 4.

2Collect the companies behind the titles

ToolsApolloOcean.io (option)AI Ark (option)LinkedInSales Navigator (option)Google Sheets
  • In Apollo, paste every variation into the organization job titles filter, add the headcount band and geography, and leave industry blank. Export the companies, not the people.
  • With Ocean.io or AI Ark, run the variations as a people search grouped by domain. Apply core: lists.
  • Search the title on LinkedIn by hand for 20 to 30 companies. Under 8 in 10 real seats means the variations are too loose; cut the noisy ones and rerun.
  • Write one row per company: domain, matched title, how many people hold it, source. Four clinical educators mean a bigger program than one.

3Qualify the company from its website

ToolsFirecrawlClaudeAttio
  • The title proves the need, the website proves the fit. Scrape the home, about and product pages and have a cheap model give a yes or no ICP verdict with one reason. Record what it sells and who buys; step 6 uses both.
  • Drop the no's and anything the model could not read.
  • Apply core: exclusions. Recruiters surface often in title searches because they list client roles.

4Build the persona lanes

ToolsClaudeApolloLinkedIn
  • Lane one is always the title holder. They run the program the title proves, so the angle speaks to it directly: "you run CME courses, need more physicians in them?"
  • The other lanes are the people above and beside that seat. For a medical-education title: the CMO, the VP Sales, the director of professional education, field marketing or events, growth, business development, and the CEO under 200 people. Four to eight lanes; stop when a lane would carry the same angle as another.
  • Give each lane its own title list with 20 to 30 variations and one angle written from what the tell-tale title proves. Events gets "warm up attendees before your next show".

5Enrich and verify per lane

ToolsApolloLeadMagicProspeoMillionVerifierBounceBan
  • Pull the people lane by lane, only at companies that survived step 3, one to three per lane per company, using the decision maker rule by size from core.
  • Apply core: enrichment.
  • Apply core: verification. Keep the lane on every row; campaigns and the weekly read are split by it.

6Write and launch one campaign per lane

ToolsClaudeEmailBisonInstantly (option)HeyReach
  • Apply core: copy and send rules.
  • Open on the proof the title gives you: "your clinical education team runs courses for surgeons" is a fact, not a compliment. One offer per lane, a value CTA, and the title itself is the personalization. Never say you looked at their org chart.
  • Draft three angle variants for email 1 and one threaded nudge for email 2 per lane. Show them to the user before upload.
  • Upload leads per lane with {PROGRAM}, {AUDIENCE}, {COMPANY}, scheduled for the next business day. LinkedIn connection on day 1 for lanes one and two.

7Read by lane and feed the next segment

ToolsAttioSlackGoogle Sheets
  • Weekly: companies found, ICP-fit rate, sent, reply rate, interested rate and meetings, split by lane. A lane under half the best lane's interested rate after two full sends is paused.
  • The lanes that reply become the targeting for the next segment: keep them, pick the next tell-tale title in step 1, run again. Log the matched title on the CRM company record as a dated signal of type employs-title so later plays can stack it.
  • Apply core: credits and handoff.

Guardrails

  • A title that can exist without the need is not a tell-tale title. "Marketing Manager" proves nothing; "Director of Clinical Education" proves a program.
  • Companies first, people second. Pulling people before the website check spends credits on companies that fail the ICP.
  • Never mention the title search, the org chart or the person whose seat proved the need. The copy speaks to the program.
  • One lane per person; someone who fits two goes into the more senior one. Do not run this play and a cold industry play on the same domain in the same month.
  • Recruiters and staffing firms are the most common false positive in a title search; remove them before enrichment.

Example run

User: "/pipeline-org-chart-qualifier for our outbound agency, offer is physician outreach for companies that run accredited education, ICP is US manufacturers 51 to 2000 people that sell to clinicians"

  1. Named three tell-tale titles (Medical Education, Clinical Education, CME), expanded to 74 variations.
  2. Apollo returned 2,140 people at 486 companies; Ocean.io added 63 domains after dedupe. LinkedIn spot check on 30 companies confirmed 27 real seats.
  3. Website read gave 312 ICP yes out of 549; 19 removed under exclusions, mostly recruiters. 293 companies qualified.
  4. Eight lanes: clinical education, marketing, sales, growth, business development, product marketing, events, CEO under 200 people.
  5. 1,960 people pulled, 1,410 verified valid, 96 catch-all in their own campaigns, 454 flagged with no email and kept.
  6. Eight campaigns created, copy approved, scheduled for Monday from separate inbox sets.
  7. Week one: clinical education lane 4.1% interested, events 2.8%, product marketing 0.6% and paused. Matched title logged on 293 company records.

# Org-Chart Qualifier

Some needs show up as a job title before they show up anywhere else. A Director of Clinical Education means courses for clinicians, a Head of Partnerships means a channel, a RevOps Manager means a sales stack, a Community Manager means a community. Search for the title, collect the companies behind it, and the list is qualified by what each company actually does.

Load `pipeline-outbound-core` first. Where a step says "apply core", follow that section of the core skill.

## Inputs

Ask for these before step 1, and save them to `./pipeline-plays.config.json`:

1. The ICP in one paragraph: headcount band, geography, the problem you solve, who owns it. If the user has an ICP file, read it.
2. The offer in one sentence, one proof point, and the pain it removes. The tell-tale titles come from the pain, not the product.
3. Stack answers from core section 1, plus which people database supports an organization-level title filter. Apollo is the default.
4. A headcount cap; without one a title search returns the enterprise end of every market first.

## Steps

### 1. Name the tell-tale titles
Tools: Claude
Flow:
- Two to five titles | Each title is a job that only exists when the company has the need you solve | with: Claude | chips: title, what it proves, function it sits in
= Variations per title | 20 to 30 keyword variations each: canonical, informal, legacy, regional, abbreviation | with: Claude | chips: canonical, informal, legacy, regional

- With the user, list two to five titles whose existence proves the need, and write what each proves: "Director of Clinical Education" proves the company trains clinicians and runs courses. If a title could exist at a company that does not need you, drop it.
- Expand each title into 20 to 30 keyword variations: canonical, the informal form people type on LinkedIn, the legacy name, regional spellings, the abbreviation. Volume hides in the messy variants, so "Clinical Educator" and "Clinical Ed" count.
- Keep one row per variation with its parent title and what it proves; it is the filter for step 2 and the angle source for step 4.

### 2. Collect the companies behind the titles
Tools: Apollo, Ocean.io (option), AI Ark (option), LinkedIn, Sales Navigator (option), Google Sheets
Flow:
- Organization title filter | Paste the variations into the organization job titles filter so the result is companies that employ the title, headcount and location on top | with: Apollo | chips: variations, headcount band, location
- People search grouped by company | Run the same variations as a people search and group the rows by domain | with: Ocean.io, AI Ark | tag: option
- Manual check | Search the title on LinkedIn for 20 to 30 companies to confirm the filter finds real seats, not keyword noise | with: LinkedIn, Sales Navigator | tag: spot check
= One row per company | Domain, matched title, count of people holding it, source; the people are not kept yet | with: Google Sheets | chips: domain, matched title, function headcount, source

- In Apollo, paste every variation into the organization job titles filter, add the headcount band and geography, and leave industry blank. Export the companies, not the people.
- With Ocean.io or AI Ark, run the variations as a people search grouped by domain. Apply core: lists.
- Search the title on LinkedIn by hand for 20 to 30 companies. Under 8 in 10 real seats means the variations are too loose; cut the noisy ones and rerun.
- Write one row per company: domain, matched title, how many people hold it, source. Four clinical educators mean a bigger program than one.

### 3. Qualify the company from its website
Tools: Firecrawl, Claude, Attio
Flow:
- Website read | Scrape the home, about and product pages and ask a cheap model for a yes or no ICP verdict with one reason | with: Firecrawl, Claude | chips: ICP yes or no, reason, what they sell, who buys
= Exclusions | Customers, open deals, active sequences, competitors and recruiters removed before any paid lookup | with: Attio | tag: core: exclusions

- The title proves the need, the website proves the fit. Scrape the home, about and product pages and have a cheap model give a yes or no ICP verdict with one reason. Record what it sells and who buys; step 6 uses both.
- Drop the no's and anything the model could not read.
- Apply core: exclusions. Recruiters surface often in title searches because they list client roles.

### 4. Build the persona lanes
Tools: Claude, Apollo, LinkedIn
Flow:
- The title holder | The person whose seat proved the need is lane one; they run the program your offer feeds | with: Claude | chips: lane 1, runs the program
- Above and beside | The people who fund the program and the peers who share its goal, four to eight lanes in total | with: Claude, Apollo, LinkedIn | chips: CMO, VP Sales, growth, BD, events, CEO under 200
= One angle per lane | Each angle written from what the title proves, in the words that lane uses for the problem | with: Claude | chips: lane, title list, angle, proof

- Lane one is always the title holder. They run the program the title proves, so the angle speaks to it directly: "you run CME courses, need more physicians in them?"
- The other lanes are the people above and beside that seat. For a medical-education title: the CMO, the VP Sales, the director of professional education, field marketing or events, growth, business development, and the CEO under 200 people. Four to eight lanes; stop when a lane would carry the same angle as another.
- Give each lane its own title list with 20 to 30 variations and one angle written from what the tell-tale title proves. Events gets "warm up attendees before your next show".

### 5. Enrich and verify per lane
Tools: Apollo, LeadMagic, Prospeo, MillionVerifier, BounceBan
Flow:
- People per lane | Pull the lane's titles at the qualified companies only, one to three people per lane per company | with: Apollo | chips: lane, company, title, LinkedIn URL
- Email waterfall | Each vendor only sees what the one before it missed | with: LeadMagic, Prospeo | tag: core: enrichment
- Two-pass verification | Valid to the lane's campaign, catch-all to its own | with: MillionVerifier, BounceBan | tag: core: verification

- Pull the people lane by lane, only at companies that survived step 3, one to three per lane per company, using the decision maker rule by size from core.
- Apply core: enrichment.
- Apply core: verification. Keep the lane on every row; campaigns and the weekly read are split by it.

### 6. Write and launch one campaign per lane
Tools: Claude, EmailBison, Instantly (option), HeyReach
Flow:
- Open on the proof | First line names the program the title proves and the result it is missing; one offer, value CTA | with: Claude | chips: {PROGRAM}, {AUDIENCE}, {COMPANY}
= Launch per lane | One campaign per lane from its own inbox set, LinkedIn connection for lanes one and two | with: EmailBison, Instantly, HeyReach | tag: send

- Apply core: copy and send rules.
- Open on the proof the title gives you: "your clinical education team runs courses for surgeons" is a fact, not a compliment. One offer per lane, a value CTA, and the title itself is the personalization. Never say you looked at their org chart.
- Draft three angle variants for email 1 and one threaded nudge for email 2 per lane. Show them to the user before upload.
- Upload leads per lane with `{PROGRAM}`, `{AUDIENCE}`, `{COMPANY}`, scheduled for the next business day. LinkedIn connection on day 1 for lanes one and two.

### 7. Read by lane and feed the next segment
Tools: Attio, Slack, Google Sheets
Flow:
= Weekly read by lane | Companies found, ICP-fit rate, sent, reply rate, interested, meetings per lane; the lanes that reply become the next targeting | with: Attio, Slack, Google Sheets | chips: lane, interested rate, matched title logged as signal

- Weekly: companies found, ICP-fit rate, sent, reply rate, interested rate and meetings, split by lane. A lane under half the best lane's interested rate after two full sends is paused.
- The lanes that reply become the targeting for the next segment: keep them, pick the next tell-tale title in step 1, run again. Log the matched title on the CRM company record as a dated signal of type `employs-title` so later plays can stack it.
- Apply core: credits and handoff.

## Guardrails

- A title that can exist without the need is not a tell-tale title. "Marketing Manager" proves nothing; "Director of Clinical Education" proves a program.
- Companies first, people second. Pulling people before the website check spends credits on companies that fail the ICP.
- Never mention the title search, the org chart or the person whose seat proved the need. The copy speaks to the program.
- One lane per person; someone who fits two goes into the more senior one. Do not run this play and a cold industry play on the same domain in the same month.
- Recruiters and staffing firms are the most common false positive in a title search; remove them before enrichment.

## Example run

User: "/pipeline-org-chart-qualifier for our outbound agency, offer is physician outreach for companies that run accredited education, ICP is US manufacturers 51 to 2000 people that sell to clinicians"

1. Named three tell-tale titles (Medical Education, Clinical Education, CME), expanded to 74 variations.
2. Apollo returned 2,140 people at 486 companies; Ocean.io added 63 domains after dedupe. LinkedIn spot check on 30 companies confirmed 27 real seats.
3. Website read gave 312 ICP yes out of 549; 19 removed under exclusions, mostly recruiters. 293 companies qualified.
4. Eight lanes: clinical education, marketing, sales, growth, business development, product marketing, events, CEO under 200 people.
5. 1,960 people pulled, 1,410 verified valid, 96 catch-all in their own campaigns, 454 flagged with no email and kept.
6. Eight campaigns created, copy approved, scheduled for Monday from separate inbox sets.
7. Week one: clinical education lane 4.1% interested, events 2.8%, product marketing 0.6% and paused. Matched title logged on 293 company records.

Play 07 · Foundation

Ad-Spend Qualifier

Trigger A store-tech filter plus live ads in the ad libraries
Outcome E-commerce list where every brand has budget
Channels email

Give this to your agent 
Install the Pipeline.tech "Ad-Spend Qualifier" outbound play. Fetch
https://raw.githubusercontent.com/pipeline-tech/plays/main/core/SKILL.md and
https://raw.githubusercontent.com/pipeline-tech/plays/main/plays/ad-spend-qualifier/SKILL.md,
save them as ~/.claude/skills/pipeline-outbound-core/SKILL.md and
~/.claude/skills/pipeline-ad-spend-qualifier/SKILL.md, then run /pipeline-ad-spend-qualifier.
Paste into Claude Code. It installs the shared core rules plus this play, then runs it as /pipeline-ad-spend-qualifier.
Workflow, generated from the skillOpen full size

E-commerce is the most crowded outbound market there is: tens of thousands of brands sit above any revenue floor, and every agency and tool vendor emails them. Two things still work, qualifying on budget before you write a word and keeping the email short enough to read on a phone. Live ad spend is the best free budget signal there is, so this play builds the list from the ad libraries up.

Load pipeline-outbound-core first. Where a step says "apply core", follow that section of the core skill.

Inputs

Ask for these before step 1, and save them to ./pipeline-plays.config.json:

  1. The ICP in one paragraph: store categories, revenue band, countries, the problem you solve and who owns it.
  2. Which platforms count: Shopify only, or Shopify plus WooCommerce and BigCommerce.
  3. The spend floor that means budget; default 10 or more active ads for 30 or more days.
  4. The offer in one sentence, one proof point, and the free thing you can make them: a welcome flow, ad creative, a teardown.
  5. Stack answers from core section 1.

Steps

1Build the brand list from a store-tech filter

ToolsStoreLeadsBuiltWith (option)Apollo (option)
  • Pull every store on the chosen platforms by category, country and revenue band. StoreLeads filters on all four and estimates monthly sales; BuiltWith covers platform and country, with traffic as the size proxy.
  • Apply core: lists. Keep platform, category and revenue band on every row; step 6 splits campaigns on them.

2Qualify on live ad spend

ToolsMeta Ad LibraryGoogle Ads TransparencySerperFirecrawl
  • Open each brand's page in the Meta Ad Library (a Serper search on brand name plus domain finds it when the store does not link it), filter to active, and record creatives running and the start date of the oldest still live. Repeat in the Google Ads Transparency Center by domain. Firecrawl or a browser script reads both at volume.
  • Band the result. None: zero active ads anywhere, so no budget for you. Light: under 10 creatives or under 30 days. Funded: 10 or more for 30 or more days, a budget and someone whose job is spending it. Heavy: 40 or more. Store counts, oldest start date and check date on every row.
  • The none band leaves the list here with a dated flag.

3Add context cheaply

ToolsStoreLeadsFirecrawlClaude
  • Add product count, review count and the installed app stack. Apps show maturity and which offer fits; a brand on Klaviyo and Recharge is a different conversation from one with no email tool.
  • A cheap model reads the homepage and returns category in two words, positioning in one sentence and a rough price point; vendor category labels are wrong often enough to split a campaign badly.
  • Apply core: exclusions, and drop marketplaces and resellers before any paid people lookup.

4Find the buyer, enrich and verify

ToolsApolloLeadMagicProspeoMillionVerifierBounceBan
  • Under about 50 people, the founder or owner. Above that, the head of growth, ecommerce director or CMO, whoever owns the channel your offer touches. One contact per brand, two at most.
  • Apply core: enrichment.
  • Apply core: verification. A founder with only a personal address goes to a separate lane, only after the business address has failed both verifiers, and sends from its own campaign.

5Write 15 to 30 word emails

ToolsClaude
  • Apply core: copy and send rules, with one override: email 1 is 15 to 30 words, not 25 to 55. E-commerce buyers do not read; they want a good offer and a very short email, and every sentence spent on cleverness is one they skip.
  • Structure: the offer in one sentence, one proof point in a clause, then a value CTA asking permission to send something made for them: "Mind if I send over a free welcome flow built for {BRAND}?" No compliments about the store, no quoting their ad count, no sign you looked at their ads. Never a call on email 1; a booking link only after a yes.
  • Email 2 is a one-line threaded nudge three days later; its only job is a second look at email 1.
  • Subject lines per core. Three angle variants for email 1 plus one nudge, approved by the user before upload.

6Launch per band and category

ToolsEmailBisonInstantly (option)Attio
  • Apply core: send rules. One campaign per category and spend band, so beauty funded and beauty heavy read on their own. Never mix bands; the point of step 2 is to learn which band buys.
  • Upload with {BRAND} and {CATEGORY}, schedule the next business day, and write every contact to the CRM with play, spend band, ad count, category and campaign.

7Read weekly by band

ToolsAttioSlack
  • Weekly: sent, reply rate, interested, contacts per lead and meetings, split by spend band and category. A good offer here lands one lead per few hundred contacts; a band above one per thousand is not paying for its inboxes.
  • Cut underperforming bands after two full weeks, move inboxes to bands that convert, and recycle cut brands through the TAM Rotation play. Re-run step 2 on the none band every 90 days.
  • Apply core: credits and handoff.

Guardrails

  • Zero active ads means no budget for you. Those brands never enter a campaign; they wait with a date.
  • Ad spend is a filter, never an opener. Do not quote ad counts, creative or run dates in any email. Per core, never say you were watching them.
  • Email 1 is 15 to 30 words, no exceptions: offer first, one proof point, value CTA. No call and no link.
  • One brand, one sequence, at most two contacts. Never run this and another cold play on the same domain in the same month.
  • Personal addresses live in their own campaign, only after the business address has failed both verifiers.

Example run

User: "/pipeline-ad-spend-qualifier for our Klaviyo email and SMS agency, Shopify brands in the US, beauty and supplements, 1M to 20M a year, we can build a free welcome flow"

  1. Asked for offer line and proof point, kept the default spend floor, confirmed EmailBison plus Attio plus Slack.
  2. StoreLeads returned 6,480 Shopify stores inside the band; BuiltWith added 1,120. Merged to 7,050 brands after dedupe by domain.
  3. Ad check: 2,310 with zero active ads parked, 2,460 light, 2,280 funded, of which 640 heavy.
  4. Context added for the 2,280 funded brands; 190 removed as customers, in sequence or marketplaces. 2,090 remain.
  5. 2,640 contacts found; 2,210 verified valid, 160 catch-all to their own campaign, 270 personal-address-only founders held for a later lane.
  6. Six campaigns: funded and heavy per category, plus one light band per category as a baseline. Copy approved at 22 and 26 words, scheduled for Monday.
  7. Week three read: funded at one lead per 240 contacts, heavy at one per 180, light at one per 610. Both light bands cut and sent to TAM Rotation.

name: pipeline-ad-spend-qualifier
description: Pipeline.tech play. Build an e-commerce brand list where every brand has budget. version: 0.1.0
requires: pipeline-outbound-core
trigger: A store-tech filter plus live ads in the ad libraries
outcome: E-commerce list where every brand has budget
category: foundation
channels: email
tools: StoreLeads, BuiltWith, Apollo, Meta Ad Library, Google Ads Transparency, Serper, Firecrawl, Claude, LeadMagic, Prospeo, MillionVerifier, BounceBan, EmailBison, Instantly, Attio, Slack

# Ad-Spend Qualifier

E-commerce is the most crowded outbound market there is: tens of thousands of brands sit above any revenue floor, and every agency and tool vendor emails them. Two things still work, qualifying on budget before you write a word and keeping the email short enough to read on a phone. Live ad spend is the best free budget signal there is, so this play builds the list from the ad libraries up.

Load `pipeline-outbound-core` first. Where a step says "apply core", follow that section of the core skill.

## Inputs

Ask for these before step 1, and save them to `./pipeline-plays.config.json`:

1. The ICP in one paragraph: store categories, revenue band, countries, the problem you solve and who owns it.
2. Which platforms count: Shopify only, or Shopify plus WooCommerce and BigCommerce.
3. The spend floor that means budget; default 10 or more active ads for 30 or more days.
4. The offer in one sentence, one proof point, and the free thing you can make them: a welcome flow, ad creative, a teardown.
5. Stack answers from core section 1.

## Steps

### 1. Build the brand list from a store-tech filter
Tools: StoreLeads, BuiltWith (option), Apollo (option)
Flow:
- Store-tech filter | Shopify, WooCommerce and BigCommerce stores by category, country and estimated revenue band | with: StoreLeads, BuiltWith | chips: platform, category, country, revenue band | tag: list source
- Database tech filter | The same platform filter inside Apollo when the user already pays for it; counts run lower | with: Apollo | tag: option
= One row per brand | Merged and deduped by domain, raw pull kept with a source column | chips: domain, brand, platform, category, revenue band, source

- Pull every store on the chosen platforms by category, country and revenue band. StoreLeads filters on all four and estimates monthly sales; BuiltWith covers platform and country, with traffic as the size proxy.
- Apply core: lists. Keep platform, category and revenue band on every row; step 6 splits campaigns on them.

### 2. Qualify on live ad spend
Tools: Meta Ad Library, Google Ads Transparency, Serper, Firecrawl
Flow:
- Meta active ads | Find the brand page, filter to active, count creatives and read the start date of the oldest | with: Meta Ad Library, Serper | chips: active count, oldest start date, platforms
- Google active ads | Search the domain, same two numbers | with: Google Ads Transparency, Firecrawl | chips: active count, first shown
= Spend band | None, light or funded; 10 plus active ads for 30 plus days is funded and zero is out | chips: none, light, funded, heavy

- Open each brand's page in the Meta Ad Library (a Serper search on brand name plus domain finds it when the store does not link it), filter to active, and record creatives running and the start date of the oldest still live. Repeat in the Google Ads Transparency Center by domain. Firecrawl or a browser script reads both at volume.
- Band the result. None: zero active ads anywhere, so no budget for you. Light: under 10 creatives or under 30 days. Funded: 10 or more for 30 or more days, a budget and someone whose job is spending it. Heavy: 40 or more. Store counts, oldest start date and check date on every row.
- The none band leaves the list here with a dated flag.

### 3. Add context cheaply
Tools: StoreLeads, Firecrawl, Claude
Flow:
- Store size signals | Product count, review count and the app stack: Klaviyo, Gorgias, Recharge and the like | with: StoreLeads | chips: products, reviews, apps
- Homepage read | A cheap model names the category and the positioning in one line each | with: Firecrawl, Claude | chips: category, positioning, price point | tag: core: exclusions

- Add product count, review count and the installed app stack. Apps show maturity and which offer fits; a brand on Klaviyo and Recharge is a different conversation from one with no email tool.
- A cheap model reads the homepage and returns category in two words, positioning in one sentence and a rough price point; vendor category labels are wrong often enough to split a campaign badly.
- Apply core: exclusions, and drop marketplaces and resellers before any paid people lookup.

### 4. Find the buyer, enrich and verify
Tools: Apollo, LeadMagic, Prospeo, MillionVerifier, BounceBan
Flow:
- Buyer by size | Founder or owner under about 50 people; head of growth, ecommerce director or CMO above that | with: Apollo | chips: founder, head of growth, ecommerce director, CMO
- Email waterfall | Each vendor only sees the contacts the one before it missed | with: LeadMagic, Prospeo | tag: core: enrichment
- Two-pass verification | Valid to the main campaign, catch-all to its own campaign | with: MillionVerifier, BounceBan | tag: core: verification

- Under about 50 people, the founder or owner. Above that, the head of growth, ecommerce director or CMO, whoever owns the channel your offer touches. One contact per brand, two at most.
- Apply core: enrichment.
- Apply core: verification. A founder with only a personal address goes to a separate lane, only after the business address has failed both verifiers, and sends from its own campaign.

### 5. Write 15 to 30 word emails
Tools: Claude
Flow:
- Offer first | One sentence of offer and one proof point; no personalization tricks and no data call-outs | with: Claude | chips: {BRAND}, {CATEGORY}, offer, proof
- Value CTA | Ask permission to send something made for them; never a call on email 1 | with: Claude | tag: value CTA
= Email 2 | One line that puts email 1 back on top | with: Claude | chips: 3 day delay, threaded

- Apply core: copy and send rules, with one override: email 1 is 15 to 30 words, not 25 to 55. E-commerce buyers do not read; they want a good offer and a very short email, and every sentence spent on cleverness is one they skip.
- Structure: the offer in one sentence, one proof point in a clause, then a value CTA asking permission to send something made for them: "Mind if I send over a free welcome flow built for {BRAND}?" No compliments about the store, no quoting their ad count, no sign you looked at their ads. Never a call on email 1; a booking link only after a yes.
- Email 2 is a one-line threaded nudge three days later; its only job is a second look at email 1.
- Subject lines per core. Three angle variants for email 1 plus one nudge, approved by the user before upload.

### 6. Launch per band and category
Tools: EmailBison, Instantly (option), Attio
Flow:
= One campaign per category and spend band | Own inbox set, scheduled to the next business day, each row written to the CRM with play, band and category | with: EmailBison, Instantly, Attio | tag: send

- Apply core: send rules. One campaign per category and spend band, so beauty funded and beauty heavy read on their own. Never mix bands; the point of step 2 is to learn which band buys.
- Upload with `{BRAND}` and `{CATEGORY}`, schedule the next business day, and write every contact to the CRM with play, spend band, ad count, category and campaign.

### 7. Read weekly by band
Tools: Attio, Slack
Flow:
= Weekly read by spend band | Sent, reply rate, interested, contacts per lead and meetings, split by band and category; cut what underperforms | with: Attio, Slack

- Weekly: sent, reply rate, interested, contacts per lead and meetings, split by spend band and category. A good offer here lands one lead per few hundred contacts; a band above one per thousand is not paying for its inboxes.
- Cut underperforming bands after two full weeks, move inboxes to bands that convert, and recycle cut brands through the TAM Rotation play. Re-run step 2 on the none band every 90 days.
- Apply core: credits and handoff.

## Guardrails

- Zero active ads means no budget for you. Those brands never enter a campaign; they wait with a date.
- Ad spend is a filter, never an opener. Do not quote ad counts, creative or run dates in any email. Per core, never say you were watching them.
- Email 1 is 15 to 30 words, no exceptions: offer first, one proof point, value CTA. No call and no link.
- One brand, one sequence, at most two contacts. Never run this and another cold play on the same domain in the same month.
- Personal addresses live in their own campaign, only after the business address has failed both verifiers.

## Example run

User: "/pipeline-ad-spend-qualifier for our Klaviyo email and SMS agency, Shopify brands in the US, beauty and supplements, 1M to 20M a year, we can build a free welcome flow"

1. Asked for offer line and proof point, kept the default spend floor, confirmed EmailBison plus Attio plus Slack.
2. StoreLeads returned 6,480 Shopify stores inside the band; BuiltWith added 1,120. Merged to 7,050 brands after dedupe by domain.
3. Ad check: 2,310 with zero active ads parked, 2,460 light, 2,280 funded, of which 640 heavy.
4. Context added for the 2,280 funded brands; 190 removed as customers, in sequence or marketplaces. 2,090 remain.
5. 2,640 contacts found; 2,210 verified valid, 160 catch-all to their own campaign, 270 personal-address-only founders held for a later lane.
6. Six campaigns: funded and heavy per category, plus one light band per category as a baseline. Copy approved at 22 and 26 words, scheduled for Monday.
7. Week three read: funded at one lead per 240 contacts, heavy at one per 180, light at one per 610. Both light bands cut and sent to TAM Rotation.

Full-TAM Cold Email

Trigger A qualified TAM and one offer per segment
Outcome Qualified replies in your CRM every week
Channels email

Give this to your agent 
Install the Pipeline.tech "Full-TAM Cold Email" outbound play. Fetch
https://raw.githubusercontent.com/pipeline-tech/plays/main/core/SKILL.md and
https://raw.githubusercontent.com/pipeline-tech/plays/main/plays/full-tam-cold-email/SKILL.md,
save them as ~/.claude/skills/pipeline-outbound-core/SKILL.md and
~/.claude/skills/pipeline-full-tam-cold-email/SKILL.md, then run /pipeline-full-tam-cold-email.
Paste into Claude Code. It installs the shared core rules plus this play, then runs it as /pipeline-full-tam-cold-email.
Workflow, generated from the skillOpen full size

One broad campaign to a whole market tells you nothing when it underperforms, because every variable moved at once. Split the same list into cells where industry, persona and receiving mail provider are each held constant, and every campaign becomes its own read. Cut the cells that fail, move volume into the ones that work, and the TAM becomes a weekly stream of qualified replies.

Load pipeline-outbound-core first. Where a step says "apply core", follow that section of the core skill.

Inputs

Ask for these before step 1, and save them to ./pipeline-plays.config.json:

  1. The qualified TAM: output of pipeline-icp-from-won-deals or pipeline-full-tam-map, or a CSV with email, first name, company domain, industry and job title.
  2. The ICP in one paragraph, naming up to three industries and three buyer personas.
  3. One offer per segment: the thing you will send or make for them, plus one proof point. One offer for everything means fewer cells.
  4. Stack answers from core section 1.

Steps

1Qualify and clean the list

ToolsClaudeAttioEmailBison
  • Apply core: lists.
  • Apply core: exclusions. Also strip anyone your sender has ever emailed unless the brief allows retargets. This play is net-new by default.
  • Cap each company at 10 contacts per wave, most senior first; park the rest.
  • Clean merge fields now: title-case names, strip legal suffixes and taglines from company names. Copy assumes clean fields.

2Split into the 27-cell matrix

ToolsEmailGuardMX lookup (option)
  • Tag every row with its receiving provider. Google MX goes to Google, Microsoft 365 to Microsoft, everything else (Proofpoint, Mimecast, custom, unknown) to Other.
  • Build the matrix: up to 3 industries x 3 personas x 3 providers. One segment per campaign, never mixed. Name campaigns {Industry} | {Persona} | {Provider}.
  • Cell size 50 to 500. Merge a cell under 50 into its neighbour on industry or persona, never on provider. Split a cell over 500 into waves.
  • The provider split uses identical copy across its three cells. It exists so you can reallocate volume mid-flight.

3Enrich and verify

ToolsLeadMagicProspeoMillionVerifierBounceBan
  • Apply core: enrichment, for rows that passed step 1 only.
  • Apply core: verification. Catch-all rows get their own campaign per cell, suffixed | Catch-all.

4Write one offer per segment

ToolsClaude
  • Apply core: copy and send rules.
  • Per industry x persona pair (up to 9), draft three angle variants for email 1. Angles differ in opener, proof and ask, not in synonyms, and share one variable set.
  • Three lines per email: their situation, value with a proof number, one ask. Open like a technician asking a diagnostic question only someone in their seat would recognise, not with a stat or a promise.
  • The ask on email 1 is a value CTA: permission to send something made for them (a list, a mockup, a short video). Never a call. Any later booking link is a "strategy session", never an "audit".
  • Email 2 is one deliberately simple threaded nudge after 3 days. Its only job is a second look; "are you the right person for this" is fine. Reuse no distinctive keyword from email 1.
  • Get approval on the plain-text angles before anything is spintaxed.

5Spintax and QA

ToolsClaudeQA checklist
  • Spintax the approved source only. Same angle count in and out; reword, never extend. Render neighbouring spintax blocks together and fix any combination that reads broken.
  • Subject block per fresh send follows core: copy and send rules.
  • Banned on every spintax path: free, guaranteed, profits, offer, quote, earn, #1. Use "on us" or "put in writing" instead.
  • QA gate before upload: plain text on, open tracking off, 2 steps, body 25 to 55 words excluding sign-off, no greeting prefix, no em dashes, variables populated on every lead, inboxes connected with warmup score 90 or higher. Zero blockers or no launch.

6Upload and launch

ToolsEmailBisonInstantly (option)Attio
  • Create the segment variables first, then upload leads with per-segment values ({SEGMENT_SITUATION}, {SEGMENT_HOOK}). In the EmailBison API, custom variables are posted as {"name": "X", "value": "Y"} and the first step waits 1 day minimum.
  • One campaign per cell, same copy across the three provider cells, its own inbox set. Attach senders (paginate the list), apply the schedule, add the two-step sequence, activate.
  • Apply core: CRM and alerts, with the cell name on every contact.
  • Preview three rendered emails per cell with real lead data first.

7Read results weekly and move volume

ToolsEmailBisonAttioSlack
  • Per cell, log sent, bounce, reply rate, interested, meetings, and emails per interested lead (E2L). Under 1:300 excellent, about 1:500 workable, over 1:1,000 weak. Optimise E2L, not reply volume.
  • Diagnostic chain, in order: bounce above 3% indicts verification (pause, re-verify). Low reply rate indicts the list. Low interested rate with normal replies indicts segment and offer. Interested leads that never become meetings indict the handover.
  • Compare providers. Microsoft often produces near-zero qualified interest. Move its daily volume to the Google and Other cells rather than rewriting.
  • Phase 1 settles the offer across the three angles. Phase 2 holds the winner and tests wording. Phase 3 scales. Success is 2% positive reply or 20% of replies positive. A variant that lifts replies but lowers meetings loses.
  • One or two cells usually carry the run. Scale those, pause the rest, refill with the parked contacts from step 1.
  • Apply core: credits and handoff.

Guardrails

  • Never mix two industries, two personas or two providers in one campaign, even to hit a minimum size.
  • No third step. A new angle launches as a new campaign to the untouched part of the cell.
  • Never ask for a call in email 1.
  • A reply is not a result. Read interested rate and meetings before naming a winner.
  • No retargets inside 30 days, and strip replied, bounced and blacklisted rows before every new wave.

Example run

User: "/pipeline-full-tam-cold-email with the TAM CSV from last week, we sell outsourced bookkeeping to clinics, construction firms and agencies"

  1. Loaded 6,340 companies, 5,120 after the ICP check and exclusions, 7,480 contacts after the 10-per-firm cap.
  2. Tagged 3,910 Google, 2,260 Microsoft, 1,310 Other. 27 cells, 4 under 50 merged into neighbouring personas, leaving 23 campaigns plus 5 catch-all campaigns.
  3. 5,860 verified valid, 410 catch-all kept.
  4. Nine offers approved, 27 angles and 9 nudges spintaxed, 11 broken combinations fixed, QA gate clean on all 28 campaigns.
  5. Launched Monday on 36 inboxes. Week 3: 9,800 sent, 0.9% bounce, 1.8% reply, 22 interested, E2L 1:445. Google 1:290, Microsoft 1:1,630. Microsoft volume moved to Google and Other; clinics x practice manager carried 11 of 22 interested and moved to Phase 2.
The skill fileplays/full-tam-cold-email/SKILL.mdExactly what gets installed. Copy it, save it as ~/.claude/skills/pipeline-full-tam-cold-email/SKILL.md next to the core skill, and run /pipeline-full-tam-cold-email.

name: pipeline-full-tam-cold-email

USE WHEN user says "/pipeline-full-tam-cold-email" OR "full TAM cold email" OR "27-cell campaign" OR "split campaigns by email provider" OR "segment and launch this list".

# Full-TAM Cold Email

One broad campaign to a whole market tells you nothing when it underperforms, because every variable moved at once. Split the same list into cells where industry, persona and receiving mail provider are each held constant, and every campaign becomes its own read. Cut the cells that fail, move volume into the ones that work, and the TAM becomes a weekly stream of qualified replies.

Load `pipeline-outbound-core` first. Where a step says "apply core", follow that section of the core skill.

## Inputs

Ask for these before step 1, and save them to `./pipeline-plays.config.json`:

1. The qualified TAM: output of `pipeline-icp-from-won-deals` or `pipeline-full-tam-map`, or a CSV with email, first name, company domain, industry and job title.
2. The ICP in one paragraph, naming up to three industries and three buyer personas.
3. One offer per segment: the thing you will send or make for them, plus one proof point. One offer for everything means fewer cells.
4. Stack answers from core section 1.

## Steps

### 1. Qualify and clean the list
Tools: Claude, Attio, EmailBison
Flow:
- ICP check and exclusions | Website-read fit check; customers, open deals and anyone the sender has ever emailed come off | with: Claude, Attio, EmailBison | chips: net-new only, 10 contacts per firm
- Clean merge fields | Title-case names, legal suffixes and taglines stripped before copy assumes them | chips: first name, company, title

- Apply core: lists.
- Apply core: exclusions. Also strip anyone your sender has ever emailed unless the brief allows retargets. This play is net-new by default.
- Cap each company at 10 contacts per wave, most senior first; park the rest.
- Clean merge fields now: title-case names, strip legal suffixes and taglines from company names. Copy assumes clean fields.

### 2. Split into the 27-cell matrix
Tools: EmailGuard, MX lookup (option)
Flow:
- Tag the receiving provider | The MX record decides Google, Microsoft or Other | with: EmailGuard, MX lookup
= Up to 3 industries x 3 personas x 3 providers | One segment per campaign, never mixed; cells of 50 to 500, merged on industry or persona, never on provider | chips: Industry | Persona | Provider, identical copy across providers

- Tag every row with its receiving provider. Google MX goes to Google, Microsoft 365 to Microsoft, everything else (Proofpoint, Mimecast, custom, unknown) to Other.
- Build the matrix: up to 3 industries x 3 personas x 3 providers. One segment per campaign, never mixed. Name campaigns `{Industry} | {Persona} | {Provider}`.
- Cell size 50 to 500. Merge a cell under 50 into its neighbour on industry or persona, never on provider. Split a cell over 500 into waves.
- The provider split uses identical copy across its three cells. It exists so you can reallocate volume mid-flight.

### 3. Enrich and verify
Tools: LeadMagic, Prospeo, MillionVerifier, BounceBan
Flow:
- Email waterfall | Only for rows that passed step 1 | with: LeadMagic, Prospeo | tag: core: enrichment
- Two passes | Catch-all rows get their own campaign per cell | with: MillionVerifier, BounceBan | tag: core: verification

- Apply core: enrichment, for rows that passed step 1 only.
- Apply core: verification. Catch-all rows get their own campaign per cell, suffixed `| Catch-all`.

### 4. Write one offer per segment
Tools: Claude
Flow:
= Three angles per industry x persona pair | Their situation, value with a proof number, one value ask; never a call on email 1; email 2 is one simple threaded nudge | with: Claude | chips: email 1 x3, threaded nudge, one variable set, strategy session not audit

- Apply core: copy and send rules.
- Per industry x persona pair (up to 9), draft three angle variants for email 1. Angles differ in opener, proof and ask, not in synonyms, and share one variable set.
- Three lines per email: their situation, value with a proof number, one ask. Open like a technician asking a diagnostic question only someone in their seat would recognise, not with a stat or a promise.
- The ask on email 1 is a value CTA: permission to send something made for them (a list, a mockup, a short video). Never a call. Any later booking link is a "strategy session", never an "audit".
- Email 2 is one deliberately simple threaded nudge after 3 days. Its only job is a second look; "are you the right person for this" is fine. Reuse no distinctive keyword from email 1.
- Get approval on the plain-text angles before anything is spintaxed.

### 5. Spintax and QA
Tools: Claude, QA checklist
Flow:
- Spintax the approved source | 20 or more subject options, banned words out, neighbouring blocks rendered and fixed | with: Claude | chips: free, guaranteed, offer, quote, earn
- QA gate | Plain text on, open tracking off, 2 steps, 25 to 55 words, variables populated, warmup score 90 or higher | with: QA checklist | tag: zero blockers

- Spintax the approved source only. Same angle count in and out; reword, never extend. Render neighbouring spintax blocks together and fix any combination that reads broken.
- Subject block per fresh send follows core: copy and send rules.
- Banned on every spintax path: free, guaranteed, profits, offer, quote, earn, #1. Use "on us" or "put in writing" instead.
- QA gate before upload: plain text on, open tracking off, 2 steps, body 25 to 55 words excluding sign-off, no greeting prefix, no em dashes, variables populated on every lead, inboxes connected with warmup score 90 or higher. Zero blockers or no launch.

### 6. Upload and launch
Tools: EmailBison, Instantly (option), Attio
Flow:
= One campaign per cell, its own inbox set | Segment variables first, then leads with per-segment values, then the two-step sequence, then activate | with: EmailBison, Instantly, Attio | chips: {SEGMENT_SITUATION}, {SEGMENT_HOOK}, 3 previews per cell | tag: send

- Create the segment variables first, then upload leads with per-segment values (`{SEGMENT_SITUATION}`, `{SEGMENT_HOOK}`). In the EmailBison API, custom variables are posted as `{"name": "X", "value": "Y"}` and the first step waits 1 day minimum.
- One campaign per cell, same copy across the three provider cells, its own inbox set. Attach senders (paginate the list), apply the schedule, add the two-step sequence, activate.
- Apply core: CRM and alerts, with the cell name on every contact.
- Preview three rendered emails per cell with real lead data first.

### 7. Read results weekly and move volume
Tools: EmailBison, Attio, Slack
Flow:
- Diagnostic chain | Bounce above 3% indicts verification, low replies the list, low interested the offer, no meetings the handover | with: EmailBison, Attio | chips: E2L under 1:300, about 1:500, over 1:1,000
- Move volume | Microsoft cells usually underperform; shift daily volume to Google and Other, scale the one or two cells that carry the run | with: Slack | tag: phase 1 to 3

- Per cell, log sent, bounce, reply rate, interested, meetings, and emails per interested lead (E2L). Under 1:300 excellent, about 1:500 workable, over 1:1,000 weak. Optimise E2L, not reply volume.
- Diagnostic chain, in order: bounce above 3% indicts verification (pause, re-verify). Low reply rate indicts the list. Low interested rate with normal replies indicts segment and offer. Interested leads that never become meetings indict the handover.
- Compare providers. Microsoft often produces near-zero qualified interest. Move its daily volume to the Google and Other cells rather than rewriting.
- Phase 1 settles the offer across the three angles. Phase 2 holds the winner and tests wording. Phase 3 scales. Success is 2% positive reply or 20% of replies positive. A variant that lifts replies but lowers meetings loses.
- One or two cells usually carry the run. Scale those, pause the rest, refill with the parked contacts from step 1.
- Apply core: credits and handoff.

## Guardrails

- Never mix two industries, two personas or two providers in one campaign, even to hit a minimum size.
- No third step. A new angle launches as a new campaign to the untouched part of the cell.
- Never ask for a call in email 1.
- A reply is not a result. Read interested rate and meetings before naming a winner.
- No retargets inside 30 days, and strip replied, bounced and blacklisted rows before every new wave.

## Example run

User: "/pipeline-full-tam-cold-email with the TAM CSV from last week, we sell outsourced bookkeeping to clinics, construction firms and agencies"

1. Loaded 6,340 companies, 5,120 after the ICP check and exclusions, 7,480 contacts after the 10-per-firm cap.
2. Tagged 3,910 Google, 2,260 Microsoft, 1,310 Other. 27 cells, 4 under 50 merged into neighbouring personas, leaving 23 campaigns plus 5 catch-all campaigns.
3. 5,860 verified valid, 410 catch-all kept.
4. Nine offers approved, 27 angles and 9 nudges spintaxed, 11 broken combinations fixed, QA gate clean on all 28 campaigns.
5. Launched Monday on 36 inboxes. Week 3: 9,800 sent, 0.9% bounce, 1.8% reply, 22 interested, E2L 1:445. Google 1:290, Microsoft 1:1,630. Microsoft volume moved to Google and Other; clinics x practice manager carried 11 of 22 interested and moved to Phase 2.

TAM Rotation

Workflow, generated from the skillOpen full size

You cannot spawn more market, so once the TAM is mapped and sent, the only question is what you do with the list the second time. Most teams treat a worked list as spent and go hunting for a fresh one that does not exist. Treat it as an asset instead: suppress, refresh, change the angle, rotate the inboxes, and the same market pays out again every 60 to 90 days.

Load pipeline-outbound-core first. Where a step says "apply core", follow that section of the core skill.

Inputs

Ask for these before step 1, and save them to ./pipeline-plays.config.json:

  1. The worked list from pipeline-full-tam-cold-email or any list sent once, with cell name, send date and reply bucket per contact.
  2. The reply export per campaign, classified into the core buckets.
  3. The current offer per segment and the phase each cell reached.
  4. Inbox inventory: live and reserve batches with warmup scores.
  5. Stack answers from core section 1.

Steps

1Audit the last cycle

ToolsEmailBisonAttioClaudeGoogle Sheets
  • Per cell, log sent, bounce, reply rate, interested, meetings and emails per interested lead (E2L). Rank the cells; one or two usually carried the run and the aggregate hides it.
  • Read every reply, not only the interested ones. Group objections and questions into a counted list; each line feeds step 3.
  • Profile the repliers: title, company size, sub-vertical, receiving provider. Note what they share that non-responders do not.
  • One sheet, one row per cell, so cycles compare line by line.

2Suppress and refresh the list

ToolsAttioHubSpot (option)EmailBisonLeadMagicProspeoMillionVerifierBounceBan
  • Apply core: lists. Apply core: exclusions. Then remove every replier in any bucket, customers and open deals, opt-outs, hard bounces and anyone contacted in the last 90 days. The pool is non-responders only.
  • Re-enrich what remains, monthly: confirm each contact is still in seat, replace movers with the current role holder, add decision makers who joined since the last pull. Apply core: enrichment.
  • Apply core: verification on the whole pool, including rows that were valid last cycle. Addresses die between cycles.
  • Keep suppressed rows with a reason column. "Not now" replies return on their recall date, not on the cycle.

3Draft the next experiment

ToolsClaudeSerperGoogle SheetsSlack
  • Write each experiment as hypothesis, segment, copy: "price objectors respond to a cost-per-outcome angle; construction x owner; three variants plus a threaded nudge."
  • Three inputs feed it: the objection list from step 1 (answer the most common one in the opener), account news via Serper dated inside 90 days, and the traits of the repliers (write for the seat and size that answered).
  • Phase rule: phase 1 settles the offer, phase 2 holds the offer and tests wording, phase 3 scales. Cells do not skip a phase between cycles.
  • Success is 2% positive reply or 20% of replies positive. A variant that lifts replies but lowers meetings loses.
  • Apply core: copy and send rules. Post the plain-text experiments to the user. Nothing is spintaxed or uploaded until each one has a yes.

4Add volume you do not have yet

ToolsApolloOcean.ioFirecrawlLeadMagicProspeoHeyReach (option)
  • Adjacent titles. At every account in the pool, add the role one seat over or one level down. Their "forwarding this to the right person" is the internal referral the first cycle never got.
  • Adjacent vertical. Pick one vertical explicitly outside the ICP that shares the problem. A proxy and web-data client found agencies, not its target market, converting on a lane nobody expected to work. Run it as one high-volume backup campaign. If it lands under 1 lead per 500 emails, investigate why and promote it to a full segment next cycle.
  • Public-inbox lane. Scrape info@, hello@ and contact@ from each company's website, never guess them. It runs as its own campaign because results conflict across offers: one source saw 2 to 4% reply and 10 to 15% positive, mostly low-urgency "sure, why not" replies; another offer saw role emails fail to convert at all. Keep or cut it per offer on its own numbers.
  • Tier 2 contacts in the adjacent-title lane also get a LinkedIn connection request two days after email 1. Apply core: tiering.

5Rotate the infrastructure

ToolsEmailBisonInstantly (option)EmailGuard
  • Apply core: send rules. Pause any campaign above 3% bounce.
  • Hold a reserve at a 1 to 1 ratio. Month 1 batch A sends while batch B warms; month 2 they swap. A cycle never launches from the batch that just finished.
  • Check blocklists daily. A listed domain is replaced, not nursed: the redirect moves to a fresh secondary domain and its inboxes rebuild from warmup.
  • Any campaign paused two weeks or more is re-verified before it resumes.

6Relaunch the non-responders

ToolsEmailBisonInstantly (option)HeyReach (option)AttioClaude
  • Spintax the approved experiments and run the pipeline-full-tam-cold-email step 5 QA gate. Zero blockers or no launch.
  • One campaign per cell as before, cell name plus a cycle suffix, from the rested batch. Step 4 lanes get their own campaigns and inboxes so they read alone.
  • Never the same email twice to the same person. If the new angle shares opener, proof or ask with the cycle 1 angle for that cell, rewrite before upload.
  • Apply core: CRM and alerts, with cycle number, cell and angle on every contact.

7Read and schedule the next cycle

ToolsEmailBisonAttioSlackGoogle Sheets
  • Weekly diagnostic chain: bounce indicts verification, low replies the list, low interested the offer, no meetings the handover.
  • Compare each cell against its last-cycle line. Improved cells move to the next phase; cells that failed twice are retired until the offer changes.
  • Judge the added lanes alone: promote the adjacent vertical under 1:500, keep the public-inbox lane only if it clears the success threshold.
  • Rhythm: copy rotates every 4 to 8 weeks, the list re-enriches monthly, the whole market turns over every 60 to 90 days. Book the next audit before this cycle finishes.
  • Apply core: credits and handoff.

Guardrails

  • Never send the same email twice to the same person, across cycles or lanes.
  • Non-responders only. A reply in any bucket removes the contact from rotation; recalls return on their own date.
  • No experiment sends without the user's approval on the plain text.
  • The public-inbox lane never shares a campaign with decision-maker contacts.
  • Never launch from the batch that just finished, or from any inbox under its 14-day warmup.

Example run

User: "/pipeline-tam-rotation on the bookkeeping TAM, cycle 1 finished ten weeks ago"

  1. Audit: 23 campaigns, 21,400 sent, 1.6% reply, 48 interested, 19 meetings, E2L 1:446. Two cells carried 31 of 48. Objections: 14 "we have a bookkeeper", 9 "not the right person", 6 on price.
  2. 7,480 contacts, 1,180 suppressed. Re-enrichment replaced 612 movers and added 290 decision makers. 6,710 verified valid, 380 catch-all kept.
  3. Three experiments approved: clinics answer "we have a bookkeeper" with a month-end-close angle (phase 2), construction holds the offer and tests proof wording, agencies try a cash-flow angle (phase 1).
  4. Added 2,140 adjacent titles, 1,900 adjacent-vertical contacts (dental labs) and 3,300 scraped public inboxes.
  5. Batch B (36 inboxes, warmup 92 or higher) took the sends, batch A back to warmup. Two blocklisted domains replaced.
  6. 31 campaigns tagged cycle 2 launched Monday.
  7. Week 4: 14,200 sent, 0.8% bounce, 1.9% reply, 34 interested, E2L 1:418. Clinics improved to 1:260. Adjacent vertical 1:470, promoted. Public inboxes 2.6% reply, 9 interested, kept. Next audit booked eleven weeks out.

# TAM Rotation

You cannot spawn more market, so once the TAM is mapped and sent, the only question is what you do with the list the second time. Most teams treat a worked list as spent and go hunting for a fresh one that does not exist. Treat it as an asset instead: suppress, refresh, change the angle, rotate the inboxes, and the same market pays out again every 60 to 90 days.

Load `pipeline-outbound-core` first. Where a step says "apply core", follow that section of the core skill.

## Inputs

Ask for these before step 1, and save them to `./pipeline-plays.config.json`:

1. The worked list from `pipeline-full-tam-cold-email` or any list sent once, with cell name, send date and reply bucket per contact.
2. The reply export per campaign, classified into the core buckets.
3. The current offer per segment and the phase each cell reached.
4. Inbox inventory: live and reserve batches with warmup scores.
5. Stack answers from core section 1.

## Steps

### 1. Audit the last cycle
Tools: EmailBison, Attio, Claude, Google Sheets
Flow:
- Per-cell scorecard | Sent, replies, interested, meetings and emails per lead for every cell of the last cycle | with: EmailBison, Attio, Google Sheets | chips: E2L under 1:300 keep, over 1:1000 cut
- Read the replies | Objections and questions bucketed with counts; repliers profiled by title, size and sub-vertical | with: Claude | chips: what replied, what objected, which cells carried

- Per cell, log sent, bounce, reply rate, interested, meetings and emails per interested lead (E2L). Rank the cells; one or two usually carried the run and the aggregate hides it.
- Read every reply, not only the interested ones. Group objections and questions into a counted list; each line feeds step 3.
- Profile the repliers: title, company size, sub-vertical, receiving provider. Note what they share that non-responders do not.
- One sheet, one row per cell, so cycles compare line by line.

### 2. Suppress and refresh the list
Tools: Attio, HubSpot (option), EmailBison, LeadMagic, Prospeo, MillionVerifier, BounceBan
Flow:
- Suppress | Repliers in any bucket, customers, opt-outs, bounces and anyone contacted in the last 90 days come off | with: Attio, EmailBison | chips: 90-day rule, non-responders only
- Re-enrich and re-verify | Job changes caught, movers replaced, new decision makers added, then two verification passes | with: LeadMagic, Prospeo, MillionVerifier, BounceBan | tag: core: lists and exclusions

- Apply core: lists. Apply core: exclusions. Then remove every replier in any bucket, customers and open deals, opt-outs, hard bounces and anyone contacted in the last 90 days. The pool is non-responders only.
- Re-enrich what remains, monthly: confirm each contact is still in seat, replace movers with the current role holder, add decision makers who joined since the last pull. Apply core: enrichment.
- Apply core: verification on the whole pool, including rows that were valid last cycle. Addresses die between cycles.
- Keep suppressed rows with a reason column. "Not now" replies return on their recall date, not on the cycle.

### 3. Draft the next experiment
Tools: Claude, Serper, Google Sheets, Slack
Flow:
- Three inputs | Objections from the replies, news on the accounts, traits of the people who replied | with: Claude, Serper | chips: objection, news, trait
- One hypothesis per cell | A segment, a fresh angle and the phase it sits in; the user approves before anything sends | with: Slack, Google Sheets | tag: approval gate

- Write each experiment as hypothesis, segment, copy: "price objectors respond to a cost-per-outcome angle; construction x owner; three variants plus a threaded nudge."
- Three inputs feed it: the objection list from step 1 (answer the most common one in the opener), account news via Serper dated inside 90 days, and the traits of the repliers (write for the seat and size that answered).
- Phase rule: phase 1 settles the offer, phase 2 holds the offer and tests wording, phase 3 scales. Cells do not skip a phase between cycles.
- Success is 2% positive reply or 20% of replies positive. A variant that lifts replies but lowers meetings loses.
- Apply core: copy and send rules. Post the plain-text experiments to the user. Nothing is spintaxed or uploaded until each one has a yes.

### 4. Add volume you do not have yet
Tools: Apollo, Ocean.io, Firecrawl, LeadMagic, Prospeo, HeyReach (option)
Flow:
- Adjacent titles | The seat beside the buyer at the same accounts; they forward internally | with: Apollo, LeadMagic, Prospeo | chips: internal referral, same accounts
- Adjacent vertical | A vertical outside the ICP that shares the need, run as backup volume; promote it under 1 lead per 500 emails | with: Ocean.io | chips: backup lane, promote at 1:500
- Public-inbox lane | info@ and hello@ scraped from the site, never guessed, always its own campaign | with: Firecrawl | chips: scraped not guessed, own campaign

- Adjacent titles. At every account in the pool, add the role one seat over or one level down. Their "forwarding this to the right person" is the internal referral the first cycle never got.
- Adjacent vertical. Pick one vertical explicitly outside the ICP that shares the problem. A proxy and web-data client found agencies, not its target market, converting on a lane nobody expected to work. Run it as one high-volume backup campaign. If it lands under 1 lead per 500 emails, investigate why and promote it to a full segment next cycle.
- Public-inbox lane. Scrape info@, hello@ and contact@ from each company's website, never guess them. It runs as its own campaign because results conflict across offers: one source saw 2 to 4% reply and 10 to 15% positive, mostly low-urgency "sure, why not" replies; another offer saw role emails fail to convert at all. Keep or cut it per offer on its own numbers.
- Tier 2 contacts in the adjacent-title lane also get a LinkedIn connection request two days after email 1. Apply core: tiering.

### 5. Rotate the infrastructure
Tools: EmailBison, Instantly (option), EmailGuard
Flow:
- Reserve at 1 to 1 | For every live inbox a reserve inbox is warming; batches swap month over month | with: EmailBison | chips: batch A sends, batch B warms, swap monthly
- Daily health | Blocklist checks, burned domains replaced, pause above 3% bounce, re-verify after a 2-week pause | with: EmailGuard | tag: core: send rules

- Apply core: send rules. Pause any campaign above 3% bounce.
- Hold a reserve at a 1 to 1 ratio. Month 1 batch A sends while batch B warms; month 2 they swap. A cycle never launches from the batch that just finished.
- Check blocklists daily. A listed domain is replaced, not nursed: the redirect moves to a fresh secondary domain and its inboxes rebuild from warmup.
- Any campaign paused two weeks or more is re-verified before it resumes.

### 6. Relaunch the non-responders
Tools: EmailBison, Instantly (option), HeyReach (option), Attio, Claude
Flow:
= One campaign per cell, fresh angle, rested inbox batch | Non-responders only, never the same email twice to one person, cycle number on every contact | with: EmailBison, HeyReach, Attio | chips: cycle 2, new angle, rested inboxes | tag: send

- Spintax the approved experiments and run the `pipeline-full-tam-cold-email` step 5 QA gate. Zero blockers or no launch.
- One campaign per cell as before, cell name plus a cycle suffix, from the rested batch. Step 4 lanes get their own campaigns and inboxes so they read alone.
- Never the same email twice to the same person. If the new angle shares opener, proof or ask with the cycle 1 angle for that cell, rewrite before upload.
- Apply core: CRM and alerts, with cycle number, cell and angle on every contact.

### 7. Read and schedule the next cycle
Tools: EmailBison, Attio, Slack, Google Sheets
Flow:
- Cycle against cycle | The step 1 sheet with this cycle beside the last one per cell | with: EmailBison, Attio, Google Sheets | chips: E2L, interested, meetings
- Rhythm | Copy every 4 to 8 weeks, list monthly, whole market every 60 to 90 days | with: Slack | chips: copy 4 to 8 weeks, list monthly, market 60 to 90 days

- Weekly diagnostic chain: bounce indicts verification, low replies the list, low interested the offer, no meetings the handover.
- Compare each cell against its last-cycle line. Improved cells move to the next phase; cells that failed twice are retired until the offer changes.
- Judge the added lanes alone: promote the adjacent vertical under 1:500, keep the public-inbox lane only if it clears the success threshold.
- Rhythm: copy rotates every 4 to 8 weeks, the list re-enriches monthly, the whole market turns over every 60 to 90 days. Book the next audit before this cycle finishes.
- Apply core: credits and handoff.

## Guardrails

- Never send the same email twice to the same person, across cycles or lanes.
- Non-responders only. A reply in any bucket removes the contact from rotation; recalls return on their own date.
- No experiment sends without the user's approval on the plain text.
- The public-inbox lane never shares a campaign with decision-maker contacts.
- Never launch from the batch that just finished, or from any inbox under its 14-day warmup.

## Example run

User: "/pipeline-tam-rotation on the bookkeeping TAM, cycle 1 finished ten weeks ago"
